# Physical Description of the Volume

The provided image displays the **spine** of a historical bound volume. The binding is composed of brown leather, showing significant signs of age and wear, particularly at the head and tail of the spine. 

### Binding Details
*   **Material:** Brown calfskin or goatskin leather.
*   **Decoration:** The left and right edges of the spine feature decorative gold-tooled borders. These borders consist of a repeating chevron or zigzag pattern, a style common in bookbinding from the 17th through the 19th centuries.
*   **Condition:** The leather is scuffed and worn, indicating frequent use or age-related deterioration.

### Page Edges
The edges of the text block are visible from the side. The paper has aged to a light brown or tan hue. There are faint traces of blue or grey staining on the edges, which may be the remains of **marbled edges** <note>A decorative technique where paper edges are dipped in floating pigments to create a stone-like pattern</note> or simple edge-staining used to protect the paper from dust and light.

**Vocabulary**
*   **Binding:** The material used to fasten the pages of a book together and provide a protective cover.
*   **Fore-edge:** The edge of a book's leaves opposite the spine.
*   **Gold-tooling:** Decorative patterns pressed into leather using gold leaf and heated tools.
*   **Chevron:** A V-shaped mark or symbol, often used in heraldry or decorative patterns.
*   **Leather-bound:** A book covered in animal hide, typically calf or sheepskin, for durability and prestige.

<note>The following sections modernize the Latin inscriptions found within the twelve circular medallions surrounding the central image, which represent the stages of the Great Work.</note>

**1.** **Dissolution.** <note>original: "Solutio"</note> The female never procreates with the male except in peace.

**2.** **The West.** <note>original: "Occidentem"</note> One runs down toward the East; the other toward the West.

**3.** **Divine and Bright.** <note>original: "Divinum et fulgor"</note> This is the matter which contains within itself the "living silver" <term>quicksilver or mercury</term>.

**4.** By a physical calling. In the spiritual fire. Through **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrefactionem"; the stage of decay or "the crow's head"</note>.
<margin>The Dead Head <note>original: "Caput Mortui"; the useless residue left after distillation</note></margin>

**5.** Blessed benefits. Which shall deliver me and lead this man back into the light.

**6.** **Philosophical.** The Philosopher. In the fire of the sun and the moon. There are three Mercuries.

**7.** There are three Mercuries: two are double, and there is a third.

**8.** **Fermentation.** The medium which prepares and purifies.

**9.** **Solar.** The King from the diamond <note>original: "ademate"</note>, crowned by the Moon.

**10.** **Redness.** <note>original: "Rubeo"</note> **Marriage.** It is achieved in fire, and another, and another.

**11.** **Bodies** prepared and purified in fire, and in water, and in air.

**12.** The Rule of **Whiteness**. <note>original: "Albedinis Regimen"</note> In fire, and in air, and in water, and in earth.

This Canon of *Bridlington* <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was an English Augustinian canon and a famous alchemist.</note> flourished in the days of *Edward* the Fourth, King of *England*, to whom he wrote an *Epistle* <note>original: "Epistle"; a formal letter</note>, as appears in the beginning of this *Book*. He was a true *Artist* <note>In this context, "Artist" refers to a skilled practitioner of alchemy.</note>, as everyone who has attained knowledge in this *Mastery* <note>original: "Mastery"; the secret art of the Philosopher's Stone</note> can testify. He wrote, among other *Writings*, these *Twelve Gates* of *Alchemy*,

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Bridlington, Edward IV, Twelve Gates of Alchemy, Alchemical Mastery, Preface</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<header>The Author's Preface.</header>

*The Author's Preface.*

I shall unfold his system, along with the Preface, the Recapitulation, the Erroneous Experiments he warns against, his *Letter* <note>original: "Epistle"</note> to the King, his *Vision*, and his *Wheel*.

Regarding his experience in this field, he was eminent. Indeed, in my opinion, his writings—because of their completeness and his excellent descriptions of things—are to be preferred over any that I have read or seen, and I have seen many.

I would not wish to detract from any honest, well-deserving author, but would rather candidly give them their due credit; yet *Ripley* <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist and author of 'The Compound of Alchymy.'</note> seems to me to take the prize <note>original: "carry the Garland"</note>.

For my own part, I have reason to honor *Bernard Trevisan* <note>Bernard, Count of Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist known for his lifelong search for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, who is very clever in all his writings, but especially in that letter of his to *Thomas of Bologna* <note>original: "Thomas of Bononia"</note>, in which...

<summary>The author introduces a collection of alchemical works, specifically praising the clarity and expertise of George Ripley and Bernard Trevisan over other writers in the field.</summary>
<keywords>George Ripley, Bernard Trevisan, Thomas of Bologna, alchemy, chemical philosophy, history of science</keywords>

# The Author's Preface.

Regarding this, let me seriously profess that I received the primary light in this hidden secret <note>original: "Secret"; likely referring to the Philosopher's Stone or the Great Work of alchemy</note> from a certain source. I shall not name the place, but read the Epistle, and read it again and again; for in it is most excellent truth, and naked truth.

Next to him—or rather before him in some respects—is an author whom I will not name. Yet truly, all chemical <note>original: "Chymical"</note> writers are to be preferred by any man according to how much he benefits from them. One person commends Raymond Lully <note>A 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and logician to whom many later alchemical works were attributed</note> above all others, yet I do not remember that I ever gained any benefit from reading him. In truth, some who are not professional practitioners of this secret write more edifyingly for the instruction of a beginner <note>original: "Tyro"</note> than those whom skill has made crafty—especially in those passages where they intend nothing 

<summary>The author discusses his influences in the study of alchemy, emphasizing that clarity for a beginner is often found in unexpected authors rather than famous figures like Raymond Lully.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Raymond Lully, chemical writers, secret, instruction, Tyro</keywords>

# The Author's Preface.

It was no small task to discover such secrets. I learned the secret of the **Philosophers' Magnet** <note>original: *Magnes*; a substance believed to attract the "world soul" or essential spirit into the alchemical work</note> from one source; of their **Magical Steel** <note>original: *Chalybs*; representing a purified form of iron or a specific starting material</note> from another; the use of **Diana's Doves** <note>a symbolic term for the white, sublimated vapors or "shining" stage of the matter</note> from a third; the **Air**, or rather the **Chameleon of the Philosophers** <note>referring to the changing colors of the matter during the process</note> from another; the gross preparation of their **Solvent** <note>original: *Menstruum*</note> in another; and the number of **Eagles** <note>referring to the number of distillations or sublimations required</note> in another. 

But for the actual operation on the true Matter, and the signs of the true **Mercury**, I know of no author like **Ripley** <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist</note>, though **Flamel** <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418), a French scrivener and legendary alchemist</note> is also eminent. I know what I am saying, as I know the truth and what is error through my own experiments.

For my own part, I have had experience with misleading, deceptive writers <note>original: "Sophistical Writers"</note>, and I have performed many tiresome and laborious experiments, even though I am still young. Therefore, having at length, through the un—

<summary>The author recounts his journey through various alchemical teachings, citing specific symbolic processes he learned from different masters before endorsing George Ripley as the most reliable authority.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Philosophers' Magnet, Diana's Doves, George Ripley, Nicolas Flamel, Mercury, Alchemical Experimentation</keywords>

# The Author's Preface.

Having, by the undeserved mercy of God, arrived at my Haven of Rest, I shall stretch out my hand to those who are still behind.

I have written several treatises, some in *English*, but especially in *Latin*. One *English* treatise concerning the *Stone* <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>, which was written very plainly but not finished, unfortunately slipped out of my hands; it may perhaps be published to the *world*. If it is, I would be sorry. I also wrote two *Latin* tracts: one titled *A Brief Guide to the Celestial Ruby* <note>original: "Brevis manuductio ad Rubinum Coelestem"</note>, and another, *The Fountain of Chemical Philosophy* <note>original: "Fons Chymicæ Philosophiæ"</note>. For specific reasons known only to myself, I have resolved to suppress these. I recently wrote two other *Latin* treatises: one titled *The Art of the Transformation of Metals* <note>original: "Ars Metallorum Metamorphoseas"</note>, and the other, *An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>original: "Introitus apertus ad occlusum Regis Palatium"</note>.

<summary>The author lists his various alchemical writings in English and Latin, noting that some were lost or suppressed, while introducing his most significant recent works on the transformation of metals.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Latin treatises, bibliography, Eirenaeus Philalethes, transmutation</keywords>

<header>The Author’s Preface.</header>

which perhaps you may enjoy. I wrote two *English* poems declaring the whole *Secret* <note>The "Secret" refers to the Great Work of Alchemy, the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, which are lost. I also wrote an *Enchiridion* <note>original: "Enchiridion"; a small handbook or manual</note> of *Experiments*, together with a *Diurnal* <note>original: "Diurnal"; a daily journal or diary</note> *of Meditations*. In these were many *Philosophical Recipes* declaring the whole *Secret*, with an *Enigma* <note>original: "Ænigma"; a riddle or coded alchemical puzzle</note> attached; these also fell into the hands of people who, I believe, will never return them. This last work was written in *English*, along with many others which I wrote for my own recreation and afterwards burned.

But now at last, studying how to benefit the *Sons of Art* <note>A traditional term for practitioners of alchemy</note> to my utmost ability, I have instead resolved to unfold *Ripley’s Knots* <note>Refers to the "Twelve Gates" or difficult metaphorical "knots" in the writings of the famous 15th-century English alchemist George Ripley</note>. In this way, you may have two *Witnesses* in one; for by the unfolding of his work, you shall see both the depth of the

## *The Author's Preface*

Man, and discern that both he and I were truly, and not deceptively <note>original: "Sophistically"</note>, entrusted with this Divine Science and Art. In this field, it is not merely theoretical <note>original: "notional"</note>, as many men conceive the Art to be, but consists of real experiments of Nature. These were taught to me by the only God and Master of Nature, who was my guide. I have seen and created the Secret Water of the Philosophers <note>A specialized solvent in alchemy, often called 'mercury of the philosophers,' believed to dissolve gold without heat.</note>, and I have known its use by eyewitness experience in the creation of the wonderful Elixir <note>The 'Philosopher's Stone,' a substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and prolonging life.</note>. Read these writings carefully, for they are not mere fantasies; and so, with the help of the Most High, you shall attain your desire.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page serves as the author's introductory preface, establishing his authority and the divine origin of his alchemical knowledge.</meta>

# The Author's Preface.

I write so that men may see and discern that I was truly, and not deceptively <note>original: "sophistically"</note>, entrusted with this Divine Science and Art. In this field, the results are not merely theoretical as many men imagine the Art to be, but are real experiments of Nature. I was taught by the only God and Master of Nature, who was my guide. I have seen and created the Secret Water of the Philosophers <note>The "Secret Water" or "Mercurial Water" was considered the essential solvent in alchemy used to dissolve gold and silver</note>, and I have known its salt through firsthand experience <note>original: "ocular experience"</note> in completing this admirable Work. Read these writings carefully, for they are not useless <note>original: "barren"</note>; and so, with the help of the Most High, you shall achieve your desire.

<summary>The author asserts that his alchemical knowledge is a "Divine Science" granted by God and proven through successful physical experiments, specifically the creation of the "Secret Water."</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Divine Science, Secret Water, Philosophers' Salt, Experiments of Nature, Divine Revelation</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page serves as a prefatory advertisement or note to the reader regarding the sources used for the quotations in the following work.</meta>

<page-num>551</page-num>

## AN
# Advertisement.

The *Compound of Alchemy* <note>original: "Compound of Alchymy"; a famous alchemical poem by George Ripley</note> which seems to be most used for quotations in this book, agrees for the most part with the edition published by Ralph Rabbards and printed at London in 1591, in quarto. In that edition, the English is not as archaic as the copy published by Elias Ashmole, Esq. <gloss>a</gloss> <margin>a In his most excellent and laborious collection of our ancient English Hermetic <note>Hermetic: relating to the mystical and alchemical tradition of Hermes Trismegistus</note> authors, called *Theatrum Chymicum Britannicum*, printed at London, 1652.</margin> Yet I humbly believe that this *Expositor* <note>Expositor: the author or commentator of the current work</note> has used that version along with his learned and ingenious observations upon the same. 

And I heartily wish that the learned philosophers of our age could prevail upon him <note>referring to Elias Ashmole</note> to publish his second volume of that collection, which he had almost finished nearly twenty years ago (as I heard from his own mouth). It has lain dormant ever since and is likely to remain so; for he is now unwilling to be persuaded to perfect it, unless some worthy friend of his can influence him before the sleep of death seizes him. Otherwise, those rare pieces of antiquity will be inevitably lost, to the prejudice of all philosophers and the great dishonor of the English nation.

<summary>The author explains that the quotations from George Ripley's "Compound of Alchemy" follow the 1591 Ralph Rabbards edition and urges the publication of Elias Ashmole's unfinished second volume of English alchemical texts.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, George Ripley, Ralph Rabbards, Elias Ashmole, Theatrum Chymicum Britannicum, Hermeticism, book history, English antiquities</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page serves as an introductory "Advertisement" or note to the reader regarding the editorial choices made in modernizing and correcting the text.</meta>

<margin>The style for which they [strive]</margin>
I thought it appropriate to clarify the meaning of the old English verse by changing a few words to terms more meaningful in our current speech. Nevertheless, these changes undoubtedly do not differ from the original intent of the author. I would not have presumed to alter these words, except that I felt I owed it to the author’s legacy to let the updated versions stand. Furthermore, anyone who wishes to see the specific differences may easily compare all three versions together, as they are all available in print. In performing this service, I hope I have done my duty faithfully and wronged no one.

I also take the liberty to inform the reader that in the *Exposition* <note>A detailed explanation or commentary</note> upon Sir George Ripley's *Preface*, found in the *Learned Sophies' Feast* <note>original: "Sophies Feast"; likely referring to a collection of alchemical wisdom</note>, page 52, line 5, I found the word **"greatest."** Based on what follows in line 11, I humbly suggest (with all due submission) that it should actually be **"meanest"** <note>In this context, "meanest" signifies the lowest, humblest, or most common</note>. This becomes more clearly apparent from the same author in his work *Secrets Revealed*, pages 62 and 63, and in Sir George Ripley's *5th Gate*, Stanza <note>original: "Stave"</note> 40, line 6. However, because I found the phrase "greatest price" in two separate copies, I have left it as it was and, by this notice, submit it to the reader's own judgment. I have likewise found that in the...

<summary>The editor explains their decision to modernize archaic English vocabulary for clarity and notes a potential printing error regarding the word "greatest" versus "meanest" in Sir George Ripley's alchemical writings.</summary>

### The Contents.
1. The Author's Preface.
2. An Exposition <note>original: "Exposition"; a detailed explanation or commentary</note> upon the Epistle <note>original: "Epistle"; a formal letter</note> to King Edward.

## Sir GEORGE RIPLEY'S
# EPISTLE
### TO
## King Edward the Fourth,
## EXPLAINED. <note>original: "UNFOLDED"</note>

This Epistle, as it was directly written to a King who was, in his generation, both wise and valiant, so it also contains the whole secret <note>The "secret" refers to the Great Work of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the art. It is both learnedly described and yet skillfully <note>original: "artificially"</note> concealed <note>original: "vailed"</note>. Yet, as the Author testifies that in this Epistle he does plainly untie the main knot <note>The "main knot" refers to the central difficulty or mystery of the alchemical process.</note>, so I can and do testify along with him that there is nothing more desirable for the

<summary>This page introduces a commentary on Sir George Ripley’s alchemical letter to King Edward IV, claiming the text reveals the "whole secret" of the craft despite its coded language.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, King Edward IV, alchemy, the secret, the main knot, Hermetic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Sir George Ripley's discourse on the nature of Mercury and the reduction of metals into their primary liquid state for the Alchemical Work.</meta>

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle

That only which is internal and central to the *Mercury*—which internal *Sulphur* of *Mercury* no corrosive can touch—is what we seek. Next to this method of universal *reduction* <note>original: "Reduction"; the process of returning a solid metal to its liquid, "mercurial" state</note>, there are also some other particular ways by which *Saturn* <note>Lead</note>, *Jupiter* <note>Tin</note>, *Antimony*, and even *Venus* <note>Copper</note> and *Mars* <note>Iron</note> may be reduced into a running *quicksilver* by the help of *salts*. 

However, because these salts are corporeal <note>having a physical, solid body</note>, they do not pierce as radically as the previously mentioned *liquor* does. Therefore, they do not strip the *Mercury* of its *Sulphur*. Instead, there is just as much *Sulphur* in this "Mercury of the Bodies" as there is in *common Mercury*. This extracted *Mercury* merely possesses specific qualities according to the nature of the *metal* or *mineral* from which it was drawn. For that reason, regarding our "Work"—which is to dissolve the perfect *species* of *metals*—it has no more power than common *Argent Vive* <note>original: "Argent Vive"; Latin for "living silver" or quicksilver/mercury</note>. 

There is, then, but one single humidity <note>moisture or liquid substance</note> which is applicable to our *Work*. This is certainly not derived from *Saturn* or *Venus*, nor is it drawn from anything which nature has formed on its own, but from a substance compounded by the *Art* of the *Philosopher*.

<summary>Ripley explains that while various metals can be reduced to quicksilver using salts, these extracts are no more effective for the Great Work than common mercury; the true solvent must be an artificially compounded substance.</summary>
<keywords>George Ripley, Alchemy, Mercury of the Bodies, Sulphur of Mercury, Reduction, Argent Vive, Seven Metals, Alchemical Art</keywords>

Weigh the Mercury which you sublimated <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note>, and the Mercury prepared by itself, and the weight of both will not nearly equal your original Mercury's weight. 

Therefore, boil up the water in which you made your washes <note>original: "Lotions"</note> until a skin forms on the surface, for that is a thick water; and in a cool place you shall find crystals. This is the salt of raw Mercury <note>original: "Mercury Crude"</note>, and is in no way fit for medicine. Yet it is a satisfaction for the artists to see how the impurities <note>original: "Heterogeneities"</note> of Mercury are discovered, which no art except the *Liquor of Alkahest* <note>original: "Liquor of Alchabest"; a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists</note> can do—and even that does it in a destructive way, not a generative way as this is. 

For this operation of ours is made between Male and Female <note>Alchemical metaphors for active/sulfuric and passive/mercurial principles</note>, within their own kind, between which there is a ferment <note>A substance that causes a chemical change or "ripening"</note> which effects that which no other thing in the world could do. --- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues Sir George Ripley’s discourse on the chemical principles of the "Great Work," specifically focusing on the necessity of fermentation and the relationship between sulfur and mercury.</meta>

## Sir George Ripley's Epistle

A most pure *Sulphur*, and out of the other, the *Mercury* of *Mercury*—which is the *Nut* <note>original: "Nut of Mercury"; likely referring to the core or essential nucleus of the substance</note> of *Mercury*—yet even with these you could effect nothing. For "fermental virtue" <note>The power of fermentation or transformation</note> is the wonder of the world; it is by this power that water becomes herbs, trees, plants, fruits, flesh, blood, stones, minerals, and everything else. Seek for this alone, and rejoice in it as a deservedly invaluable treasure. 

Now, know that fermentation does not work outside of its own kind; for example, salts do not ferment metals. Do you wish to know why some fixed alkalis <note>term: Alcalies; basic (non-acidic) salts used as solvents</note> extract a *Mercury* out of *minerals* and out of the more "imperfect" metals? <note>In alchemy, "imperfect" metals usually referred to lead, tin, copper, or iron, as opposed to "perfect" gold and silver.</note> Consider then, that in all these bodies, the *Sulphur* is not as radically mixed and united as it is in silver and gold. 

Now, *Sulphur* is related to various *alkalis* that are extraordinarily dissolved or melted with it; by this means, the *mercurial* parts are disjointed, and the *quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent vive"</note> is separated by fire. The *Mercury* separated in this way is robbed of its *Sulphur*, when in fact, what is actually required is only a purification <note>term: depuration</note> of the *Sulphur* by separating the impure from the pure.

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that the power of fermentation is the essential key to alchemical transformation and warns that mercury must be purified rather than stripped of its sulfur.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, alchemy, fermentation, Sulphur, Mercury, Alkalis, Argent vive, purification, metallurgy</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the chemical separation of elements within metals, specifically focusing on why common chemical processes fail to produce the true "Mercury" needed for the Alchemical Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

But these salts, having separated the *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance.</note>, leave the *Mercury* <note>Mercury represents the fluid, metallic, or "spirit" principle.</note> in a worse state. That is, it is more estranged from a metallic nature than it was before. In its original composition, that *Sulphur* of *Saturn* <note>Saturn is the alchemical name for Lead.</note> will not burn; even if it is sublimed <note>turned into vapor and solidified</note>, calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note>, made into "sugar" <note>likely referring to Sugar of Lead/Lead Acetate</note>, or vitrified <note>turned into glass</note>, it still returns to its original state when treated with fire and fluxes. 

However, once its *Sulphur* has been separated (as previously mentioned), it will catch fire if joined with saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, just as common sulfur does. Thus, the salts act upon the *Sulphur*, robbing the *Mercury* of it; but they do not act upon the *Mercury* itself because they lack a "ferment." Such a ferment can only be found among homogeneous <note>original: "Homogeneal"; meaning things of the same kind or nature</note> things. 

Therefore, the ferment used for bread cannot leaven a stone, nor does the ferment of any animal or vegetable substance ferment a metal or mineral. So then, even if you could obtain a *Mercury* out of gold by using the "Liquor of the First Essence <note>original: "Ens"; the fundamental or spiritual essence of a substance</note> of Salt," that *Mercury* would never accomplish our work. On the other hand, *Mercury* made out of gold by using *our* Mercury—even if there are three parts of our Mercury to one part of gold—this...

<summary>The author explains that chemical salts can strip sulphur from metals like lead, but they fail to transform the underlying mercury because they lack a "homogeneous ferment" specific to metals. He argues that only a mercury derived from gold through their specific alchemical process can achieve the desired transformation.</summary>

<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury, Saturn, Lead, Gold, Fermentation, Homogeneous, Saltpeter, Alchemy, Ens of Salt</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the alchemical composition, specifically focusing on the relationship between the "Body," "Sulphur," and "Mercury" within the work.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

In this perspective, our Body in its purity is called the *Philosophers' Luna* <note>original: "Philosophers Luna"; refers to alchemical silver or the purified feminine principle</note>, being far more pure than the imperfect *metals*. Its *Sulphur* is also as pure as the *Sulphur* of *Sol* <note>original: "Sol"; refers to alchemical gold or the masculine principle</note>, though it is not actually *Luna* <note>silver</note>, for it does not endure the fire.

Now, in the composition of these three—first, our common *Mercury*, and the two principles of our compound—there intervenes the ferment of *Luna*. Out of this ferment, even though it is a *Body*, there proceeds a specific odor. Indeed, the *weight* <note>original: "Pondus"</note> of it is often diminished if the *compound* is washed too much after it is already sufficiently clean. 

So then, the ferment of *Sol* and *Luna* intervenes in our composition. This ferment produces an offspring a thousand times more noble than itself. However, should you work on our compound *body* by a violent method using *salts*, you would obtain a *Mercury* far less noble than the *Body*; in such a process, the *Sulphur* of the *Body* would be separated and not exalted.

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle

## STANZA 11

*In the said book, the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus or Aristotle, often cited as "The Philosopher" in alchemical texts.</note> also speaks—*
*If it please Your Highness to read therein—*
*Of various Sulphurs, and especially of two,*
*And of two Mercuries joined to them indeed,*
*By which he leads those with true understanding*
*To the knowledge of the principles which are only true:*
*Both Red, Moist, Pure, and White, as I have observed,*
*Which are, nevertheless, found by very few.*

We now come to the third conclusion, which is that among all metallic and mineral <term>Sulphurs</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance.</note> there are only two that belong to our Work. These two have their <term>Mercuries</term> <note>Mercury represents the fluid, passive, or "spirit" principle.</note> essentially united with them. This is the truth of our secrets, even though we (to mislead the unwary) seem to assert the contrary. For do not think that—simply because we suggest two different paths—we actually mean...

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains to the King that despite the complexity of alchemical writings, there are only two specific types of Sulphur and Mercury essential to the true process of transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Sulphur, Mercury, The Philosopher, Red and White principles, Metallic principles</keywords>

as we say; for truly (as *Ripley* <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist and author of *The Compound of Alchymy*.</note> witnesses), there is no true principle but one, nor do we have more than one matter, nor but one way of working upon that matter, nor but one regimen of heat, and one linear way of proceeding.

These two *Sulphurs*, as they are principles of our work, ought to be homogeneous <note>original: "Homogeneal"; meaning of the same kind or nature.</note>, for it is only *Spiritual Gold* that we seek; *First White*, then *Red*. This gold is no other than that which the common people see, but they do not know the hidden spirit that is within it. This principle lacks nothing but composition, and this composition must be made with our other crude white *Sulphur*, which is nothing but *common Mercury* <note>original: "Mercury vulgar"; likely referring to quicksilver prepared in a specific way for the work.</note>, by frequent redistillation <note>original: "cohobation"; the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid off the solid matter from which it was drawn.</note> of it upon our *Hermaphroditic body* <note>A term for a substance containing both male/active and female/passive principles, often associated with the "Rebis."</note>, until it becomes a fiery water.

Know therefore, that *Mercury* has within itself a *Sulphur* which is inactive; our art is to multiply within it a living, active *Sulphur*. This comes out of the loins of our *Hermaphroditic body*, whose father is a *Metal* and whose mother is a *Mineral*. Take then the most beloved daughter of *Saturn* <note>In alchemy, Saturn usually represents Lead or the initial "black" stage of the work.</note>, whose arms are a *Silver Circle* <note>original: "Circle Argent."</note>,

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle

<page-num>23</page-num>

They frustrate their own hopes and then cast the blame upon the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or Hermetic Philosophy.</note>, when indeed it is only to be imputed to their own folly in not understanding the Philosophers.

I know many pitiful sophisters <note>original: "Sophiſters"; here meaning those who use clever but fallacious arguments or false practitioners of alchemy.</note> do dote on many stones: vegetable, animal, and mineral. Some even add to those the fiery, angelical, and paradisiacal stone, which they call a "wonder-working essence." Because the mark they aim at is so great, they also make the ways by which they would attain their goal equally grand—that is, a double way. 

One way they call the *Moist Way* <note>original: "Via Humida"</note>; the other they call the *Dry Way* <note>original: "Via Sicca"</note> (to use their language). They describe the latter as the *Labyrinthine path*, which is fit only for the great ones of the earth to tread in; the other they call the *Daedalean Path* <note>A reference to Daedalus, the mythical craftsman; implying a clever or intricate but accessible shortcut.</note>, an easy way of small cost for the poor of the world to undertake.

But this I know, and can testify: there is but one way, and only one *Method of Treatment* <note>original: "Regimen"</note>. There are no more colors than ours; and whatever we say or write otherwise is only to deceive the unwary. For if everything in the world ought to have its proper causes, there cannot be any one end which...

<summary>Sir George Ripley warns against "sophisters" who claim there are multiple types of Alchemical Stones or different paths (Moist and Dry) to the Great Work, asserting instead that there is only one true method.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Via Humida, Via Sicca, Regimen, Alchemical paths, Sophistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the dual nature of the "work" and the symbolic transformation of the matter.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

which is produced from two ways of working on distinct principles.

Therefore we protest, and must again admonish the reader, that (in our former writings) we have concealed much, by reason of the two ways we have insinuated, which we will briefly touch upon. 

There is one work of ours, which is the *play of children* and the *work of women* <note>original: "Play of Children" and "Work of Women"; common alchemical metaphors for a process that requires patience and gentle heat rather than complex labor</note>, and that is decoction <note>term: decoction; the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</note> by the fire. We protest that the lowest degree of this our work is that the matter be stirred up and may hourly circulate without fear of breaking the vessel, which for this reason ought to be very strong. 

But our lineal decoction is an *internal work*, which advances every day and hour and is distinct from that of outward heat; therefore, it is both invisible and imperceptible. In this our work, our *Diana* <note>Diana is a symbolic name for the purified, white stage of the alchemical matter</note> is our body when it is mixed with the water, for then all is called the *Moon*. For *Laton* <note>term: Laton; an impure metal or the "black" stage of the matter that must be cleansed</note> is whitened, and the woman bears rule. 

Our *Diana* has a wood, for in the first days of the Stone, our body—after it is whitened—grows vegetably <note>meaning it begins to sprout or "grow" like a plant within the vessel</note>. In this wood are at the last found two *doves* <note>symbolizing the volatile and fixed principles reaching a state of peace or union</note>; for about 

<summary>The author distinguishes between the external application of heat and the internal, "invisible" chemical maturation of the alchemical Stone, using the symbols of Diana and the Moon to describe the whitening stage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Sir George Ripley's explanation of the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the symbolic stages of transformation and the intentional use of cryptic language to protect the secret art.</meta>

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle

At the end of three weeks, the soul of the <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" often refers to the "philosophical mercury" or the transformative liquid principle, rather than common quicksilver.</note> ascends with the soul of the dissolved gold. These are enfolded in the everlasting arms of *Venus* <note>Venus is the alchemical symbol for copper, but here it likely refers to a specific stage of chemical attraction or a green-colored state.</note>; for in this season, the mixtures are all tinted with a pure green color. These <term>Doves</term> <note>A symbolic term for the volatile vapors rising in the flask.</note> are circulated seven times, for in the number seven is perfection. Then they are left dead, for they then rise and move no more. 

Our body is then black, like a <term>Crow's Bill</term> <note>original: "Crows Bill"; a traditional alchemical term for the "nigredo" or black stage of decomposition and purification.</note>, for in this operation everything is turned to powder, blacker than the blackest. We often use passages such as these when we speak of the preparation of our *Mercury*; and we do this to deceive the simple-minded. It is also for no other end that we mix up our operations, speaking of one when we ought to speak of another. 

For if this <term>Art</term> <note>The "Great Art" or Alchemy.</note> were set down plainly, our operations would be held in contempt even by the foolish. Therefore, believe me in this: because our works are truly natural, we take the liberty to confuse the "Philosopher's work" with that which is purely "Nature's work," so that we might keep the simple in ignorance concerning...

<summary>Sir George Ripley describes the symbolic "green" and "black" stages of the alchemical process while admitting that alchemists intentionally use confusing metaphors to hide their secrets from the unworthy.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Mercury, Gold, Venus, Doves, Crow's Bill, Nigredo, Alchemical Secrecy, Philosophical Mercury</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "true Vinegar" and the chemical/alchemical marriage of substances required for the Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

concerning our true *Vinegar* <note>original: "Vinegre"; in alchemy, this refers to a specialized solvent or "mercurial water" used to dissolve metals</note>, which being unknown, their labor is wholly lost. Let me then (as a conclusion) say only this much: Take our Body, which is Gold, and our *Mercury* which is seven times sharpened <note>original: "acuated"</note> by its marriage with our Hermaphroditical body, which is a *Chaos* <note>A term for the raw, undifferentiated prime matter</note>. This is the splendor of the Soul of the God *Mars* <note>Iron</note>, in the Earth and water of *Saturn* <note>Lead</note>. Mix these two in such a weight <note>original: "Pondus"</note> as Nature requires. 

In this mixture, you have our invisible *Fires*; for in the Water, or in the *Mercury*, there is an active *Sulphur* or Mineral Fire, and in the Gold, there is a dead, passive, but yet actual *Sulphur*. Now, when that *Sulphur* of the Gold is stirred up and quickened, there is created—between the Fire of Nature which is in the Gold and the Fire against Nature which is in the *Mercury*—a Fire consisting partly of the one and partly of the other, for it partakes of both. By these two Fires thus united into one, both Corruption (which is *Humiliation*) and Generation (which is *Glorification* and *Perfection*) are caused. Now know that God alone governs this way of the *Internal* Fire,

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle

Fire—man being ignorant of its progress, and only beholding its operations through his reason—is able to discern that it is hot; that is, that it performs the actions of heat, which is decoction <note>original: "Decoction"; the process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or achieve maturity.</note>. In this Fire there is no sublimation <note>original: "Sublimation"; in alchemy, the process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into a solid.</note>, for sublimation is an exaltation <note>original: "Exaltation"; an improvement or raising of a substance's dignity or power.</note>. But this Fire is such an exaltation that it is perfection itself, and beyond it there is no further progress.

All our work, then, is only to multiply this Fire; that is, to circulate the Body until the virtue <note>virtue: the inherent power or medicinal efficacy</note> of the *Sulphur* is augmented. Again, this Fire is an invisible spirit, and therefore does not have dimensions such as "above" or "below," but is everywhere within the sphere of activity of our matter in the vessel. 

So, although the material, visible substance may sublime and ascend by the action of the elemental heat, yet this spiritual virtue is always present in that which remains at the bottom, as well as in that which is in the upper part of the vessel. For it is like the soul in the body of man, which is everywhere at the same time, and yet is bounded or confined by none.

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that the "Philosophical Fire" is a spiritual, invisible force that permeates the entire alchemical vessel, acting like a soul to perfect the matter through constant circulation.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Philosophical Fire, Decoction, Sublimation, Sulphur, Spiritual Virtue, Alchemical Vessel</keywords>

This is the basis of one <term>sophism</term> <note>original: "Sophism"; a clever but misleading argument used by alchemists to hide their secrets</note> of ours: namely, when we say that in this true <term>philosophical fire</term> there is no <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: "Sublimation"; the process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid. Here, the author implies the internal "fire" does not physically rise like steam</note>. For the fire is the life, and the life is a soul, which is not at all subject to the physical dimensions of bodies. 

Hence also it is that the opening of the glass, or the cooling of the same during the time of working, kills the life or fire that is in this secret <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, "Sulphur" often represents the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance</note>; and yet, not one grain of the matter is lost. The <term>elemental fire</term> <note>Common, physical fire</note> then is that which any child knows how to kindle and govern. But it is the philosopher only who is able to discern the true inward fire; for it is a wonderful thing which acts in the body, yet is no part of the body. 

Therefore, the fire is a <term>celestial virtue</term> <note>original: "Coælestial Virtue"; a heavenly or spiritual power</note>; it is "uninformed"—that is, it is always the same until the period of its operation is complete. And then, having come to perfection, it acts no more; for every agent rests when the end of its action is reached.

Remember then, that when we speak of our fire which does not sublime, you must not mistake and think that the moisture of the <term>compound</term> which is within the...

### Sir George Ripley's Epistle

The contents of the glass vessel ought not to sublime <note>original: "Sublime"; to convert a solid into vapor by heat</note>, for that it must do incessantly; but the fire that does not cause sublimation is the "metallic love," which is above, below, and in all places alike. 

Now, to conclude all that has been said: learn and be well-advised what matter you take in hand, for "an evil crow lays an evil egg," as the proverb says. Let your seed be pure, and your matrix <note>original: "Matrix"; the womb or vessel where the substance is transformed</note> also be pure; then you shall see a noble offspring. 

Let the external fire be of such a temperature that our chemical mixtures <note>original: "Confections"</note> may move to and fro incessantly. In a few days, this will produce that which you most long for: the **Crow’s Bill** <note>The "Crow's Head" or "Crow's Bill" refers to *nigredo*, the first stage of alchemy characterized by blackness and putrefaction</note>. Continue then your decoction <note>original: "Decoction"; the process of heating or boiling a substance</note>, and in one hundred and thirty days you shall see the **White Dove** <note>The *albedo* stage, representing purification and whitening</note>, and in ninety days more, the **Sparkling Cherubim** <note>The final stage of reddening or perfection, often associated with the color of fire or gold</note>.

## STAVE XII.

And these two things are best, he adds immediately,
For him who practices <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: "Alchymy"; here referring to the practical art of transmuting metals</note> to take:
To make our Gold and our Silver both as one,
Therefore I say, whoever wishes to make our Pearl and Ruby,
Must see to it that he does not forsake the mentioned principles:
For if the principles are true at the beginning;
And if he can also grasp them through his craft,
In the end, he truly shall not regret his work.

Thus we come to the final conclusion, which is this: if a man’s operations are regular and his principles are true, his end will be certain—namely, the Mastery <note>The "Mastery" refers to the successful completion of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. O fools and blind, who do not consider how everything in the world has its proper cause and progress in its operation! Such fools are they who seek our secret in trivial matters, and yet hope to find the Gold of Ophir <note>original: "Gold of Ophir"; a biblical reference to a region famous for its high-quality gold, used here to represent the ultimate alchemical prize</note>.

For the more exact guiding of your practice, take notice of these *Twenty Rules* following.

### Rule I.
Whatever any sophister <note>original: "Sophister"; a person who uses clever but false arguments, particularly in alchemy</note> may suggest to you, or you may read in any sophistical author, let no one take you from this ground: namely, that as the end you look for is *Gold*, so let *Gold* be the subject on which you work, and none other.

### Rule II.
Let no one deceive you by telling you that our *Gold* is not common gold, but "Philosophical Gold." It is true that *common gold* is dead; but as we prepare it, there is a quickening <note>original: "quickening"; a bringing to life or animation</note> of it, just as a grain of corn <note>original: "Corn"; in this period, "corn" referred to any cereal grain, such as wheat</note> in the earth is quickened.

<summary>Sir George Ripley begins a list of twenty rules for alchemical practice, emphasizing that true alchemy must begin with actual gold rather than inferior materials or purely theoretical "philosophical" substances.</summary>

So then in our work, after six weeks, **Gold** that was dead becomes quick <note>original: "quick"; meaning alive or active</note>, living, and spermatic <note>capable of reproduction or growth</note>. In our composition, it may be called **Our Gold**, because it is joined with an Agent that will certainly quicken it. In the same way, a condemned man is called a "dead man," even though he is currently living.

### Rule III.
Besides Gold, which is the Body or Male, you must have another sperm, which is the Spirit and Soul, or Female; and this is **Mercury**, in flow and form like common **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argent Vive"</note>, yet more clean and pure.

There are many who, instead of Mercury, will use strange waters or liquors which they style by the name of **Philosophical Mercury**. Do not be deceived by them, for what a man sows, that he must expect to reap. If you sow your Body in any earth except that which is metallic and homogeneous <note>of the same kind or nature</note> to it, you shall—instead of a **Metallic Elixir**—reap an unprofitable **Lime** <note>original: "Calx"; a powdery metallic residue left after heating</note>, which will be of no value.

### Rule IV.

### Rule IV.
Our *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" often refers to a specialized "philosophical" mercury rather than the common liquid metal.</note> is in substance the same as common *Quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent Vive"</note>, but far different in *Form*. For it has a celestial, fiery form of excellent *power* <note>original: "Virtue"</note>; and this is the nature which it receives through our artificial preparation.

### Rule V.
The whole secret of our preparation is this: take that *mineral* which is most closely related to *gold* and to *mercury*. Impregnate <note>To saturate or fertilize.</note> this with *volatile gold*, which is found in the "reins" <note>The "reins" or kidneys; here referring to the core or interior of the metal.</note> of *Mars* <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note>. With this, purify your *mercury* until seven cycles have passed; then it is ready for the *King's Bath* <note>A metaphor for the chemical solution used to dissolve gold.</note>.

### Rule VI.
Yet know that from seven times up to ten, the *mercury* is made better and better, and becomes more active. With each preparation, it is activated by our true *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, masculine, or combustible principle.</note>. However, if it exceeds this number of preparations, it becomes too fiery; instead of dissolving the *Body* <note>The "Body" usually refers to the solid metal, such as gold, being worked upon.</note>, it will solidify <note>original: "Coagulate"</note> itself.

### Rule VII.

### Rule VII.
This *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" often refers to a specially prepared "philosophical" mercury rather than common quicksilver.</note> thus activated <note>original: "acuated," meaning sharpened or made chemically active</note>, is afterward to be distilled in a glass retort <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note> two or three times. This is done for the following reason: some atoms of the "Body" <note>The "Body" usually refers to the metal being acted upon, likely the gold mentioned in the next rule.</note> may remain in it, which were imperceptibly left behind during the preparation of the *Mercury*. Afterward, it is to be cleaned well with *vinegar* and *sal ammoniac* <note>Ammonium chloride, a salt used as a cleaning agent and flux in metallurgy.</note>; then it is ready for the work.

### Rule VIII.
Choose your *Gold* for this work pure and clean from any mixture. If it is not pure when you buy it, make it so by purification <note>original: "Purgation"</note>. Then, let it be made fine, either by filing, hammering into thin sheets <note>original: "Malleating"</note>, calcining <note>Reducing a metal to a powder through heat or corrosion.</note> with corrosive substances, or any other way by which it may be made most subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" means finely divided or reduced to its smallest possible particles.</note>.

### Rule IX.
Now proceed to your mixture. Take one ounce of the aforementioned "Body" (Gold) so chosen and prepared, and two ounces—or three at the most—of the *Mercury* animated as taught above. Mix them on a marble slab <note>original: "in a Marble"</note> which should be warmed as hot as water can heat it. Grind both together until they are well incorporated; then wash the mixture

## Rule X.

Know now, that whatever we say out of envy <note>In alchemical texts, "envy" or "jealousy" refers to the tradition of intentionally concealing secrets from the unworthy.</note>, our way is no other than this. We protest, and will continue to protest, that neither we nor any of the ancients knew any other way; for it is impossible that our secret can be wrought by any other principles, or any other disposition than this. 

Our sophism <note>A deliberate deception or "blind" used to hide the truth from uninitiated readers.</note> lies only in the two kinds of fire in our work. The internal secret fire, which is God's instrument, has no qualities perceptible to man. We often speak of that fire, yet we seem to be speaking of the external heat; and from this, many errors arise among the unwary. This internal fire is what is graduated <note>Increased in stages or intensity.</note>. As for the external heat, it is almost linear <note>Constant or uniform.</note> throughout the work; until the "white work" <note>The *albedo* stage, where the matter turns white, signaling the penultimate phase of the Great Work.</note> is achieved, it remains the same without alteration, except that in the first seven days we keep the heat a little slack for the sake of certainty and security—though an experienced philosopher need not do so. But the internal governing heat is imperceptibly <note>original: "insensibly"</note>

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that the primary "deception" in alchemical writings involves the distinction between the constant external furnace heat and the graduated internal "secret fire" of the matter itself.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Rule 10, internal fire, external heat, dulcify, white work, alchemical secrecy, sophism</keywords>

As the heat is sensibly increased hour by hour, and as the substance is daily strengthened by the continued boiling <note>original: "Decoction"; the process of heating or boiling a substance in a liquid</note>, the colors are altered and the compound is ripened. I have untied a major knot for you here; take care not to be ensnared by confusion again.

### Rule XI.

Then you must provide a glass vessel <note>original: "Tun"</note> in which you may perfect your work, for without it you could never accomplish anything. Let it be either oval or spherical, and large enough in proportion to your compound that it may hold about twelve times the quantity of the material within its globe. Let your glass be thick and strong, clear, and free of flaws, with a neck about a span or a foot long. Put your matter into this "egg" <note>a common alchemical term for the oval-shaped glass maturation vessel</note>, sealing the neck carefully so there is no flaw, crack, or hole; for even the smallest vent will let out the subtle spirit <note>the volatile or gaseous essence of the chemical</note> and destroy the work.

You may test the exact sealing of your glass in this way: when it is cold, put the neck where it is sealed into your mouth and suck strongly. If there is the slightest vent, you will draw out the air that is inside...

## Rule XII.

You must then provide yourself with a furnace, called by wise men an *Athanor* <note>term: A self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant heat over long periods.</note>, in which you may accomplish your work. No ordinary furnace will serve for your first work; you need one in which you can produce a heat that is "obscurely red" <note>A dull red heat, indicating a specific temperature range.</note> at your pleasure, or lower. At its highest degree of heat, it must be able to endure for at least twelve hours without needing more fuel.

If you wish to obtain this, observe these steps:

First, ensure that your "nest" <note>The compartment where the vessel sits.</note> is no larger than what is needed to contain your dish, with about an inch of space on the side where the vent-hole of your *Athanor* is located for the fire to circulate.

Secondly, let your dish be no larger than what is required to hold one glass vessel, with about an inch thickness of ashes between the glass and the side of the dish. Remember the saying of the philosopher: "One Glass, One Thing, One Furnace" <note>original: *Unum Vas, Una Res, Unum Furnum*. This is a fundamental alchemical maxim emphasizing the unity and isolation of the process.</note>; for such a dish standing with the

<summary>Sir George Ripley describes a physical test for the alchemical vessel and provides specific dimensions and requirements for constructing an Athanor furnace to maintain steady heat.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Athanor, alchemy, furnace construction, laboratory practice, chemical trial</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the construction and operation of a chemical furnace, specifically focusing on the management of heat and airflow.</meta>

From the bottom level to the vent-hole—which in such a furnace ought to be only one, about three inches in diameter and sloping upwards—the furnace will be kept in a constant glowing heat by the stream of flame, which is always playing toward the top of the vessel and around the bottom.

Thirdly, if your dish <note>original: "Dish"; likely a crucible or evaporating basin</note> is larger, your furnace vent must be between a third and a fourth as large as the diameter of your platter; otherwise, it cannot be heated exactly or continuously.

Fourthly, if your tower <note>original: "Tower"; the vertical section of the furnace used for fuel or draft</note> is more than six inches square at the fireplace, you are out of proportion and can never achieve the correct degree of heat. For if you cause a tower larger than that proportion to stream with flame, the heat will be too intense; and if it does not stream, the heat will not be intense enough, or only with great difficulty.

Fifthly, let the top of your furnace be closed, except for a hole just large enough for casting in coals—about three inches in diameter or square—which will powerfully keep the heat down <note>meaning: keep the heat concentrated within the furnace</note>.

### Rule XIII.

Once these things are ordered in this way, place your matter into your glass vessel and apply fire as nature requires—gentle, not too violent—beginning at the point where nature left off. Now, understand that nature has left your materials in the mineral kingdom. Therefore, although we draw comparisons from plants <note>original: "Vegetables"</note> and animals, you must understand this as a parallel within the specific kingdom where your subject is placed.

For instance, if I were to draw an analogy between the conception of a human and the growth of a plant, you must not assume that the heat required for one is the same as the heat required for the other. We know that plants will grow in the ground, which is not without heat; they feel this warmth in the earth even at the beginning of spring. Yet, an egg would not be hatched by that level of heat, nor could a man feel any warmth from it; rather, to him, it would feel like a numbing cold.

Since you know that your work pertains entirely to the mineral kingdom, you must learn what degree of heat is appropriate for mineral bodies and what may truly be called a "gentle heat" in that context.

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that alchemical heat must be proportional to the mineral kingdom, warning that "gentle heat" for a mineral is different from the heat required for plants or animals.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Rule XIII, mineral kingdom, alchemical fire, analogy of heat, laboratory practice</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the regulation of heat in the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the differences between the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

heat, and what is violent. First, now consider where Nature leaves you: not only in the Mineral Kingdom, but within it, working on Gold and *Mercury* <note>original: "Quicksilver"</note>, which are both incombustible <note>term: incombustible; incapable of being burned or consumed by fire</note>. Yet *Mercury* being delicate, will break all vessels if the fire is too extreme. Therefore, although it is incombustible—and so no fire can hurt the substance itself—it must also be kept with the Male Sperm <note>term: Male Sperm; in alchemy, this usually refers to the sulfurous principle or Gold, intended to fertilize the "mercurial" female principle</note> in one glass. If the fire is too great, this containment cannot be maintained, and consequently, the work cannot be accomplished.

So then, a temperate heat ranges from the degree of heat that will keep Lead or Tin constantly molten, up to as high as the glass will endure without danger of breaking. Thus, you begin your degrees of heat according to the Kingdom in which Nature has left you.

Just as the highest degree of heat which the root of a tree feels in the bowels of the earth is not nearly comparable to the lowest degree of heat an animal has; so the highest degree of heat a vegetable will endure without burning is too low for the first degree of Mineral heat required for our Work.

<summary>The author explains that alchemical heat must be calibrated to the Mineral Kingdom, specifically noting that while Gold and Mercury are fire-resistant, excessive heat will shatter the glass vessel and ruin the process.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Mineral Kingdom, Mercury, Gold, degrees of heat, Male Sperm, laboratory safety, transmutation</keywords>

### Sir George Ripley's Epistle

#### Rule XIV

Know that all your progress in this work is to ascend in *the mist and the clouds* <note>original: "Bus & Nubi"</note>, from the Moon up to the Sun; that is, in *the clouds* <note>original: "Nubibus"</note>. Therefore, I charge you to sublime in a continual vapor, so that the <term>Stone</term> <note>The "Stone" refers to the evolving alchemical matter or the Philosopher's Stone itself.</note> may take in air and live.

#### Rule XV

Nor is this enough; to attain our permanent <term>Tincture</term> <note>A "Tincture" is the concentrated essence or medicine capable of transforming metals.</note>, the water of our <term>Lake</term> <note>The "Lake" refers to the liquid solvent or "mercury" used in the vessel.</note> must be boiled with the <term>Ashes</term> of <term>Hermes' Tree</term> <note>A symbolic term for the solid material or "salt" remaining after a previous stage of the process.</note>. I charge you then to boil night and day without ceasing, so that in the troubles of the stormy sea, the heavenly nature may ascend and the earthly nature may descend.

For truly, if we did not boil, we would never name our work <term>Decoction</term> <note>A process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence.</note>, but rather <term>Digestion</term>. For where the <term>Spirits</term> only circulate silently, and the <term>Compound</term> below does not move by an <term>Ebullition</term> <note>The state of boiling or bubbling up.</note>, that is only properly named <term>Digestion</term>.

#### Rule XVI

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains the necessity of constant sublimation and vigorous boiling (decoction) to separate the heavenly and earthly natures within the alchemical vessel. He distinguishes between "digestion," which is a silent circulation, and "decoction," which requires active boiling.</summary>

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Sublimation, Decoction, Digestion, Hermes' Tree, Tincture, Ebullition</keywords>

### Rule XVI.
Do not be over-hasty, expecting the harvest too soon, or the end shortly after the beginning. For if you are supported by patience, in the space of fifty days at the furthest, you shall see the "Crow’s Bill." <note>The "Crow's Bill" or "Crow's Head" is an alchemical term for the black stage of the process, known as *nigredo*, which signifies the successful decomposition of the matter.</note>

Many (say the philosophers) imagine our "Solution" <note>original: *Solution*; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> to be an easy task. But how hard it truly is, only those can tell who have tried and gained experience. Do you not see a grain of corn? Sow it, and after three days you shall only see it swelled; once dried, it is the same corn it was before. Yet you cannot say it was not cast into its proper womb <note>original: *Matrix*; a place where something is generated or developed</note>, for the earth is its true place; it simply lacked its proper time to grow.

But things with a harder kernel lie in the ground a far longer time, such as nuts and plum-stones, for everything has its season. This is a true sign of a natural operation: that it waits for its season and is not rushed. Do you think, then, that gold—the most solid body in the world—will change its form in a short time? No; you must wait and wait until

## Sir George Ripley's Epistle

Until about the 40th day, utter blackness begins to appear. When you see that, then conclude your "Body" <note>In alchemy, the "Body" usually refers to the solid metallic base, often gold.</note> is destroyed—that is, made into a living Soul—and your Spirit is dead—that is, coagulated <note>original: "Coagulated"; meaning the liquid spirit has thickened and bonded with the solid.</note> with the Body. But until this sign of blackness appears, both the Gold and the *Mercury* retain their original forms and natures.

### Rule 17

Beware that your fire does not go out, even for a moment, so as to let your matter become cold; for if you do, the ruin of the work will certainly follow.

From what has been said, you may gather that all our work is nothing else but an incessant <term>boiling</term> <note>original: "boyling"</note> of your compound in the first degree of <term>liquefying heat</term>, which is found in the <term>metallic kingdom</term>. In this state, the internal <term>vapors</term> shall circulate around your matter; in this fume, the matter shall both die and be revived.

### Rule 18

Know that when the "White" <note>The *Albedo*, or white stage, which follows the black *Nigredo* stage.</note> appears, which will be about the end of five months, then the accomplishment of—

### Rule XIX.

Then continue your Fire until the colors appear again; then at last you shall see the fair Vermillion, the Red Poppy <note>The "Red Poppy" or "Vermillion" signifies the final stage, the Red Stone or Philosopher's Stone, capable of producing gold.</note>; Glorify God then, and be thankful.

### *Advertisement*

Although the rest of his <term>Adept Brethren</term> <note>original: "Adept Brethren"; referring to a secret society of alchemists who claimed to have discovered the Philosopher's Stone</note> had (as we may say, enviously) *sworn secrecy*—contrary to their accepted maxim of doing all the good they can with this large talent as long as they live, and longer if possible—yet he had not so *sworn*, even though they assumed he had. For he had, as he himself confesses, an extraordinary impulse of mind to be helpful to all *sincere searchers of this secret art* (to use his own words) and to *stretch out his hand to those who are behind*. 

Seeing, therefore, that it was the author's own desire to benefit the world by his labors, and that he gave his consent to <term>Mr. Starkey</term> <note>George Starkey (1628–1665), an American colonial alchemist and physician who published the works of Eirenaeus Philalethes</note> for printing his pieces—as appears in his preface to the *Marrow of Alchemy*—I know no reason why his *writings* should lie concealed any longer. ## Advertisement <note>original: "Advertisement"; here meaning a public notice or announcement</note>

The world was unworthy of them; nevertheless, he promised to give that person a transcript of them, but did not. This is the reason that they cannot yet be found, and their loss is very much lamented. Therefore, if any gentleman has them in his possession, or any other piece by this author, it is humbly requested that they send them to the *Pelican* <note>A well-known bookshop or publishing house identified by its sign</note> in *Little Britain*, *London*, so that they may be printed with the first *Six Gates* <note>A reference to "The Ripley Revived" or similar alchemical treatises structured by "gates" or stages</note>, which are now in the press. 

In order that I may not fail to contribute what I can toward the discovery of this author's works, I here take the liberty of presenting the reader with a catalogue of such pieces as are noted to have been written by this author under the disguised name of *Eirenaeus Philalethes* <note>A famous pseudonym meaning "a peaceful lover of truth"</note>. Part of these are listed by *Mr. Starkey* <note>George Starkey (1628–1665), an American colonial alchemist and physician who claimed to know Philalethes</note> in his aforementioned preface, and part are mentioned by the author himself, along with several others which he wrote (as he says) for his own recreation and afterwards burned. This author is acknowledged by all parties to be an Englishman and an Adept <note>term: A person who has mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy and discovered the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and is supposed to be still living and traveling, being about 55 years of age; however, his true name is not certainly known.

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

...the fountain of all goodness; I shall take up his pattern as a precedent for imitation, rather than as a subject for exposition.

And first, concerning those who shall apply themselves to this science: let them resolve that they undertake a most admirable piece of work. Although I am far from thinking that God bestows what we enjoy upon any of us for our own merits, but rather out of His free grace, let me nevertheless exhort anyone who shall direct his studies this way to address himself to the Author and Fountain of Goodness for help. Do this so that you may have grace to honor God in the use of so great a talent. For I am persuaded that whomever God shall appoint to be the heir of such a talent, He will also give him a heart to improve it correctly; otherwise, He will increase that person's judgment for the abuse of so great a blessing.

For whoever shall be wanton and dissolute, and live without the fear of God, what might he not do with such an art? That is, unless God restrains him—as He certainly will, either by hiding this secret from him or by making it a snare and a trap to betray his life into...

## *All our secrets must spring from one image.*

Just as all things had an actual existence according to their several kinds out of one mixed, confused mass <note>original: "Mafs"; referring to the "Prima Materia" or the chaotic state of matter at the beginning of creation</note>, so all these secrets must flow out of one image. Truth does not consist in heterogeneity <note>diversity or being composed of different parts</note>, but in unity; for God is one, and his works are uniform; and the more noble anything is, the nearer it is to simplicity.

*As in philosophers' books, for whoever desires to see.*

The sentences of the philosophers concur with this, as many as have truly understood the secret, as Morien <note>Morienus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist</note> often and plentifully witnesses, along with Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century Islamic chemist</note>, Trevisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century Italian alchemist</note>, and many others. The thing is but one in kind, though two in number; and though more things are used, yet until they are all brought to a oneness of nature, they are not fit to enter into this work.

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that the "Great Work" of alchemy relies on the principle of unity, arguing that just as God is one, the alchemical process must reduce diverse materials into a single, simple nature.</summary>
<keywords>George Ripley, alchemy, unity, Morienus, Geber, Bernard of Trevisan, Prima Materia, simplicity, philosophy</keywords>

*Our Stone is called the Lesser World.*

# The Preface of Sir George Ripley

### The Preface of Sir George Ripley

The spiritual, inhabiting, invisible *Sulphur* will, without any additional substances, congeal the *Mercury* in which it resides. This process creates a visible, solidified substance of *Luna* <note>Silver</note> and then *Sol* <note>Gold</note>.

&emsp;Thus, this *Trinity* is indeed a *Unity*. One part is mature *Gold*, fixed and digested in its current state; the other is volatile *Gold*, white and raw, yet (potentially) capable of being made most fixed and solid through simple digestion alone. It is not a delusion that the Philosophers speak and write of, for trust me—*they have seen Diana naked without her clothing; I speak as one who knows* <note>original: "Viderunt nudam sine veste Dianam; sciens loquor." This is an allegorical reference to witnessing the secret, pure heart of nature or the alchemical process.</note>. I know I speak the truth, which the Sons of Art <note>A traditional term for practicing alchemists</note> also know and can testify to along with me.

->*Magneșia also*<-

&emsp;This *Stone* is called by the Philosophers their *Magnesia*, their *Adrop* <note>A term for the lead or raw matter of the Stone</note>, and many other names. It is indeed their Stone in the first true mixture of the correct matter; for it is the true seed. With the cooperation of external fire and a patient expectation of Nature's timing—which is not long for one who understands it—it will produce its results.

->*Of Sulphur and Mercury.*<-

Regarding that which is performed by Nature over many years and ages in the bowels of the Earth—namely, the ripening <note>original: "decocting"</note> of *Mercury* alone without any additions—Art, in order to make the work shorter, first impregnates *Mercury* with a spiritual seed of *Sulphur*. By this process, the Mercury becomes powerful enough to dissolve metals. The Artist then adds mature *Sulphur* to it, which further shortens the work. From these two parents, which share a single root, is brought forth a Noble Son of Royal descent. This is not simply Gold, but our *Elixir*, which is ten thousand times more precious.

->*Proportioned by Nature most perfectly.*<-

Yet all this work performed by the Artist is only to assist Nature. We can do no more; indeed, we have declared, and will continue to declare, that we only serve as assistants to Nature in this matter. For all the works of God are complete and perfect; we can only behold them and admire them. Therefore, we seek our...

<summary>The author explains how alchemical Art accelerates the natural maturation of Mercury and Sulphur to create the Elixir, emphasizing that the alchemist merely assists God's perfect natural processes.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury, Elixir, Alchemy vs Nature, Decocting, Spiritual Seed, Royal Offspring</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Sir George Ripley, an influential 15th-century English alchemist, discussing the true foundations of the alchemical art.</meta>

<page-num>ii</page-num>
<header>The Preface of Sir George Ripley</header>

principles where Nature is, and amend Nature within its own nature. Nor do we make the simple believe—which is the trade of sophisters <note>original: "Sophisters"; here meaning deceptive or fraudulent practitioners</note>—that we intend to make our so highly prized Elixir through our extractions and manual operations upon vegetables, minerals, urines, hair, or the like. Instead, we use Art to make it appear out of those things in which Nature has already placed it, by revealing what was hidden and hiding what was manifest.

->But many a person marvels, and well they may, and muses on such a marvelous thing.<-

Whereas those who work upon other matters than the true substance betray their ignorance most foully; they do not consider the possibility of Nature, but work according to their own fancy. They act as though a pure, perfect metallic substance could be produced out of combustible substances that are filthy in their nature and made up of heterogeneities <note>original: "Heterogeneities"; substances consisting of dissimilar or unrelated parts</note>. Such a perfect substance is invincible by reason of its inseparable unity, and by virtue of its transcendent excellence, it cleanses and fixes all leprous and fugitive <note>original: "fugi-"; referring to "fugitive" or volatile metals like mercury that "flee" the fire</note>...

<summary>Sir George Ripley distinguishes true alchemy from the "sophistry" of those using organic waste or non-metallic minerals, arguing that the Elixir must be revealed from within Nature's own metallic principles.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Elixir, Sophisters, Nature, Metallic Substance, Transmutation</keywords>

## The Preface of Sir George Ripley

### *What is our Stone, etc.*

They marvel at the strange difficulty of the matter, nor can they almost tell how to judge what they read; forasmuch as all Philosophers say it is a very easy thing.

*For birds and fishes bring it to us; every man has it. And it is in every place—in you, in me, etc.*

And in very deed, the ancient wise men have so written, and do still write the same: namely, that it is found even in a dunghill, according to Morien <note>Morienus: a legendary 7th-century alchemist said to have taught the secrets of the Stone to the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>. Regarding the smallness of the expense, they all write plentifully; so that in respect of time and cost, Artephius and Flammel <note>Nicolas Flammel: a famous 14th-century French scribe and legendary alchemist.</note> say it is but the play of children and the work of women. Therefore, one excellent Philosopher, writing of this mastery, titles his treatise *Children's Play* <note>original: "Ludus Puerorum"</note>.

<summary>Sir George Ripley discusses the paradoxical nature of the Philosopher's Stone, noting that while it seems difficult to find, ancient masters claim it is common, inexpensive, and as simple to produce as a child's game.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Philosopher's Stone, Morien, Artephius, Nicolas Flammel, Ludus Puerorum, alchemy, easy work</keywords>

To this I answer, that it is *Mercury*, I assure you.

Yet trust me, though the wise men write this way, and it is true, there is nonetheless something to be added to their judgment, just as the author of *The New Light* <note>original: *Novum Lumen*, referring to the work of Michael Sendivogius</note> well observed: namely, that this Art is easy to him who understands it, as *Artephius* <note>A medieval alchemist often cited in Hermetic literature</note> plainly expresses. But to him who is ignorant of it, nothing can appear so difficult. "*The Wise Man*," says *Sendivogius*, "finds it in a *dunghill*, but the *fool* cannot believe that it is in *gold*."

I, for my part (through the great mercy of God to me, an unworthy and unthankful creature), know the Art to be true; and not only that, but also very easy. I wonder that men of such great talents have studied for it so long in vain. I am confident of only this: it is the gift of God. It is not achieved by him who wills it, nor by him who runs after it, but by God who shows mercy. In this respect, I am bold enough—to the glory of God—to confess that I possess the Art, and have witnessed Nature's operations in these hidden secrets before my
