But when the time comes that they should see the signs specified by the Philosophers, they fail. This may be because of something external to the *Gold* (which it picks up when it is beaten into leaves) or the *Mercury* (which it picks up during washing and purging). Although this contamination is slight, it is enough when heated to give a light <term>Tincture</term> <note>original: "Tincture"; here meaning a surface coloration or stain that mimics the appearance of the true Elixir.</note> to the surface <note>original: "Superficies"</note>. 

Following *Hogheland*, they may see a discolored exterior, which signifies nothing. Our operation is not so trivial that a man would need spectacles and a most clear light to discern it; rather, it is so apparent that even a half-blind man would be amazed at it. For our "Body"—even the perfect Body <note>In alchemy, the "perfect Body" is usually gold, which must be "opened" or "divided" by the philosophical solvent.</note>—is divided, which common *Mercury* can never do, no matter how much a man prides himself on his useless "mock-purgations" <note>Ineffective attempts to "purify" common quicksilver to make it act like the Philosophers' Mercury.</note>. 

But when such workmen have waited their time out, and perhaps waited even longer...

### Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate

conquer your Body; mollify <note>original: "mollifie"; to soften or make supple, often referring to the preparation of a metal or mineral for chemical change.</note> it, soften it, and draw out its Seed. Separate the Soul from it by the power of that Spirit which is in your dissolving Water; spirits naturally unite with spirits, just as one flame will mix with another.

The Soul being thus separated from the Body, the body will dry and rot as naturally as any other thing will once its Soul has been removed. And just as it dies and becomes putrid and black through the Water (which extracts the Soul), so by that same Water it is washed of its filthy blackness. Then the clean Soul, having cleansed the Body, is united to it, so that from that time forward the Body follows the Soul and always moves with it over the Fire, rising and descending in the form of a Spirit—which is a wonder to behold.

This is our much-esteemed secret, *Conjunction*, which is celebrated after the loosening, putrefying, and purifying of our Body. This is the true process of our Work, according to the true requirements <note>original: "exigency"</note> of Nature: first the Soul is to be divided from the Body so that the grossness may be purged by corruption and rotting, and the

the Spirit—which is a form of light and is seminal <note>original: "feminal"; meaning seed-like or containing the potential for growth</note>—can, once released, multiply itself through the Spirit of the Water. In this way, because it is allied both to the Body from which it was drawn and to the Water from whose spirit it receives an increase in power <note>original: "virtue"</note> and color <note>original: "Tincture"; in alchemy, the essence or staining power of a substance</note>, it can unite both the Spirit and the Body with a perpetual bond. He who works in this manner shall undoubtedly attain perfection.

Of two Conjunctions Philosophers make mention:
The first is "gross," when the Body is returned to a raw state <note>original: "re-incrudate"; to make raw or crude again, referring to restoring a metal to its mercurial, liquid state</note> with Mercury;
But let this pass, and pay attention to the second type,
Which is celebrated, as I said, after Separation.
In this, the parties are left with the least amount to bind together <note>original: "colligate"</note>,
And are thus promoted to a most perfect balance <note>original: "temperance"</note>,
So that there may never be any resistance <note>original: "repugnance"</note> between them again.

But when the Philosophers speak of Conjunction, one must carefully consider which Conjunction they mean; for since it is a term used very often, so...

## SIR GEORGE RIPLEY's Fourth Gate

So, this term is to be understood with great caution <note>original: "doubtfully"</note>. One type of Conjunction which the philosophers speak of is "gross" <note>meaning coarse or physical</note>, which is properly called *Amalgamation* <term>the physical process of combining mercury with another metal</term>; it is the first operation after the preparation of the *Mercury*.

But this is not the Conjunction to be understood here. This is a far more secret operation, in which man performs no work at all, but only stands by and observes Nature’s operation. This work is done without any manual intervention <note>original: "laying on of hands"</note> and happens very quickly once the materials are prepared and made ready. This work is therefore called a "Divine work."

This Conjunction is far more intimate than the "gross" version, for this is a union through the smallest parts <note>original: *per minima*</note> or through the innermost depths <note>original: *intima*</note>, so that the essence of the one enters the essence of the other, forming a single substance.

This creates a balanced mixture <term>original: "temper"; a perfect state of equilibrium between opposing qualities</term> which no human art could achieve; for just as water mixed with water is inseparable, so it is now with these principles. Now harmony, amity, and friendship are established; for now the hot and the dry will embrace the cold and moist, and now peace <note>original: "patience"; here meaning a stable state of non-resistance</note> is established between the Water and the Fire.

<meta>catchword: Thus</meta>

Thus

<summary>This page distinguishes between the "gross" physical amalgamation of mercury and the "secret" philosophical conjunction, describing the latter as a divine, natural process that creates an inseparable union of elements.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Fourth Gate, Conjunction, Amalgamation, Mercury, Nature's Operation, Divine Work, Union per minima, equilibrium, Water and Fire</keywords>

Of Water and Air, with Earth and Fire;
But so that each Element may be led into the other,
And so remain forever as you desire,
Do as plasterers <note>original: "Dawbers"; laborers who build with "wattle and daub" (mud and straw)</note> do with clay or mud <note>original: "Mire"</note>:
Mix <term>original: "Temper"; to mix to the proper consistency</term> them until they are thick, and do not make them too thin;
By doing this, you will achieve the drying process more quickly.

## Sir G. Ripley's fourth Gate.

The <term>Quadrangle</term> <note>original: a square, representing the four elements of fire, air, water, and earth</note> is turned into a <term>Circle</term> <note>a symbol of perfection and the "quintessence" or fifth element that unites the others</note>, to the amazement of Nature.

These are those principles which God has now joined together, and therefore nothing can separate them. Rejoice now, O <term>Son of Art</term> <note>a traditional title for a practitioner of alchemy</note>, for you have the <term>Sun</term> <note>symbolizing the solar/male principle or philosophical gold</note> for your <term>Diadem</term>, and the <term>Moon Crescent</term> <note>symbolizing the lunar/female principle or philosophical silver</note> for your <term>Garland</term>.

Mix them just as a <term>Potter</term> mixes his <term>Loam</term> <note>a mixture of clay, sand, and organic matter used for making bricks or pottery</note>.

### Sir G. *Ripley's fourth Gate.*
...on, I shall specify all our Conjunctions. Specifically, we have three Conjunctions, all of which must be known by anyone who intends to complete this Mastery.

The first is "gross" <note>original: "gross"; here meaning material, physical, or unrefined</note>, which I touched upon before; it is the *Amalgamation* <term>Amalgamation</term> <note>The process of mixing a metal (like gold) with mercury to form a pasty alloy</note> of *Sol* <note>original: "Sol"; the alchemical name for Gold, associated with the Sun</note> with our *Mercury*. Because the mixture is made of two things, it is called a "diptative" <note>a technical term for a twofold joining</note> Conjunction; and the compound is now called *Rebis*—that is, "two-things"—according to the verse, *A thing is double, yet made as one* <note>original: "Res Rebis est Vina confecta." The word "Vina" is likely a typographical error for the Latin "una," meaning "one"</note>.

In this mixture there are two Natures: the one more active, which is the *Mercury*, and the other more passive, which is *Gold*. Note here that the activity of the *Mercury* over the *Gold* is because the moving power of *Sol* is sealed—that is, his *Sulphur* <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the internal fire, soul, or active masculine principle of a metal</note> is imprisoned. Otherwise, when Dissolution is performed, *Sol* is then most active, and *Mercury* more passive. *Mercury* then acts as the "Feminine Sperm," which, being more raw and tender, is more quickly affected by the Fire; *Sol*, the "Masculine Sperm," does not feel the fire until it is penetrated by the *Mercury*,

## Sir George Ripley's fourth Gate.

...although it is so united that the parts ascend and descend together. The reason is that, though the Soul unites the Spirit and Body, the Spirit sometimes carries the Body upward with it, and the Body at times pulls the Spirit down <note>original: "precipitates"</note>. The Soul holds them fast together until, at last, not only these parts but also their elemental qualities are so strongly mixed together <note>original: "permixt"</note> that one does not do more in acting than the other does in resisting. By this means, they are not only united to follow one another but are fixed <note>original: "fixt"; in alchemy, "fixation" refers to making a volatile substance stable and able to endure the heat of a fire without evaporating</note> to endure fire together.

This is the last and noblest <term>Conjunction</term>, in which all the mysteries of this <term>Microcosm</term> <note>literally "little world"; the idea that the human being or the alchemical work reflects the structure of the entire universe</note> reach their completion <note>original: "Consummation"</note>. He who has arrived here may sit down at a banquet with the *Sun* and *Moon* <note>the celestial symbols for Gold and Silver, or the masculine and feminine principles of the work</note>.

This is the so highly praised <term>Stone of the Wise</term> <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>, which is entirely without fear of corruption; for here, by Nature, all...

<page-num>297</page-num>

### Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate <note>Sir George Ripley was a famous 15th-century English alchemist; his "Gates" represent the twelve stages of the alchemical process. The Fourth Gate is Conjunction.</note>
...unprofitable, since they show nothing more than quicker ways, but actually prolong the time of **Putrefaction** <note>Putrefaction is the stage where the matter decomposes and turns black, a necessary step before it can be purified and transformed.</note>. For from that time onward, the **Fire of Nature** <note>The internal, gentle heat believed to exist within the substances themselves.</note> is at work, and then every **weight** <note>original: "pondus"</note> has the same duration, provided the fire is managed accordingly and the matter in the glass vessel is not significantly more than the other. One or two ounces will be accomplished far sooner than five or six ounces; therefore, I advise everyone to content themselves with one ounce, or two at most. If you succeed with a single ounce, you can wish for nothing more.

This chapter I will conclude very soon; therefore:

*I charge you to make only one **Gross Conjunction** <note>original: "Gross Conjunction"; the initial physical mixing of the alchemical "Body" and "Water."</note>,*
*For harlots seldom bear children,*
*And likewise, you shall never obtain our **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note>,*
*Unless you let the **Woman** lie alone;*

...understand it fully; take heed then to my <term>Doctrine</term> <note>original: "Doctrine"; refers to the specific set of instructions or the theory of the alchemical process.</note>, mix your <term>Water</term> <note>The liquid solvent, often synonymous with "Philosophical Mercury."</note> with your <term>Body</term> <note>The solid material, often a purified metal like gold or silver, representing the "fixed" principle.</note> in a due quantity, and grind them together diligently; and when you have mixed them, shut them up in your <term>Glass</term> <note>The "Philosopher's Egg" or glass flask used to hold the substances during their transformation.</note> carefully, and there let them stand until complete perfection.

### Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate

Showers and dews shall be moistened, and by continual washing shall be cleansed; then through the good pleasure of God, the daylight shall spring forth, and what was before dark shall now become clear, and what was black of the blackest shall now be made very white <note>The "Albedo" or whiteness phase of the alchemical Great Work, signifying purification after the "Nigredo" or blackness phase.</note>.

Now you have come to the end of the white [phase], and you have a perfect Stone. By doing so, if you have but an ounce, you may soon have a thousand.

<sig>U 3</sig>

## Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate

Our secret consists of only two things; whatever more we seem to advise is only to entangle the unwary.

Our first secret is to know our true *sulfur*, which many writers describe using every kind of absurdity in the world. This is *gold*, which can be bought pure almost anywhere.

The next secret is to know our *mercury*. This is not common mercury, but an artificial kind drawn from three sources <note>original: "heads"</note> through the mediation of one thing. This "one thing" causes the two parts which are dry and sulfurous to unite with the one part which is moist and mercurial. These parts are different in their qualities, but our <term>decoction</term> <note>The process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or promote a chemical change.</note> reconciles this difference so as to create a sweet harmony between them.

As for the *sulfur*—the increase of whose power <note>original: "virtue"</note> is our final goal—it gives consistency to the "water" <note>the mercurial component</note>. However, it does so without separating itself from the water. Instead, with its fermenting power, it so infuses the water that what was once a moist, delicate, and <term>volatile</term> <note>In alchemy, a substance that easily evaporates or escapes the fire.</note> spirit becomes a fixed, dry, fire-abiding substance.

But first of all, the water softens <note>original: "mollifie"</note> the "body" <note>the sulfur or gold</note>, soaks into it, and searches out...

<summary>Sir George Ripley identifies the two essential components of the alchemical work as a specific "sulfur" (derived from gold) and an "artificial mercury." He explains how these opposite elements are reconciled through heating until the liquid becomes a stable, fire-resistant solid.</summary>

<header>Sir George Ripley’s Fourth Gate</header>

Where I saw a lady finely dressed,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sitting and sighing by a spring;
She spoke words as if oppressed by grief,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And often she would wring her hands.

"Ah me," she said, "how my joy has departed!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Oh dismal, cruel death!
Could anyone think the Fiend <note>In alchemical allegory, "the Fiend" or "Death" often represents the process of putrefaction, where the substance must "die" to be reborn.</note> was so flinty-hearted,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To thus deprive him of his breath?"

I then drew near, and thought I might have assuaged
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Her grief with pleasant words;
But the more I spoke, the more enraged she became—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Indeed, she even disdained relief.

Her face was lily-white, with purple spots
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Upon her cheeks and chin;
Her rose-red lips, her features free from blemishes <note>original: "blots"</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Began to fill me with amazement.

"Most rare beauty," I said, "why are you weeping?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What creature <note>original: "Wight"</note> so vile would dare
To wrong you, whom the gods keep in their care,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whose face is a snare for mortals?"

"Good sir," she said, "spare your words of sorrow;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I live, yet living, I die.
I wish my life might end before tomorrow <note>original: "marrow," an archaic spelling of "morrow" used here for the rhyme with "sorrow."</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ah, I fear that death is not near."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A king of great fame;

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That no one knew anyone like him.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Invited great honor.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And that drink <note>original: "draught"</note> brought about his end.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Made his head so light,
He could not rise, but dropped into the stream
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Into everlasting night.

He struggled to swim, but sank to the bottom—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Oh, what a dismal sight <note>original: "fight," likely an OCR error for "sight."</note> to see!
Then, swelling with the water which he drank,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He could not free himself.

## Sir George Ripley’s Fourth Gate <note>In alchemical literature, "Gates" refer to the stages of the Great Work. The Fourth Gate is typically "Putrefaction" or "Solution."</note>

But he burst, and then the spring began to boil,
And bubbling, the color changed;
It is a wonder to see (alas, the ruin!) <note>original: "spoil"</note>
The King dead, and the spring transformed. <note>original: "estrang'd"</note>

So changed from its former state, that what then shone
Like the glittering sky,
Now reeks like a stinking puddle, so that none
Can endure it; yes, even I,

Who lost a dear husband in it, do loathe
The vapors <note>The rising gasses produced during alchemical fermentation or putrefaction.</note> that ascend;
I hope one spring will be the end of both,
It would be a happy end.

She spoke for so long, until the water seemed
Like ink, it grew so black;
And also the odor <note>original: "favour"</note> that was previously <note>original: "erst"</note> considered so bad,
Far surpassed the darkness of the color. <note>original: "hew"</note>

The air was filled with the darkest clouds,
Such fogs and mists were seen,
Which severed a soul from the dead corpse,
Whose color was first green,

Then yellow, mixed with blue; the fumes ascended,
Which bore the soul on high;
When the lady saw this, her tears ended,
And she fainted immediately. <note>original: "by and by"</note>

Into the same stream she fell fainting, <note>original: "swouning"</note>
And never more appeared;
She loved her husband so, she would not stop,
Like one who was afraid of death. <note>original: "afear'd"</note>

And straightway she was of vital breath deprived; <note>The poem here splits the word "deprived" across two lines to maintain the rhyme scheme with "lived."</note>
From a lady fair,
She was made into a corpse; <note>original: "Carcass"</note> thus both who lived in love,
Did share alike in death.

Their souls, separated <note>original: "disjoyned"</note> from their bodies, hovered
Upon the fountain and played,
Waiting to see if their corpses, recovering,
Might have their lives restored. <note>original: "repaid"</note>

The sun stood at the solstice, <note>In alchemy, the "Sun in Solstice" represents the highest degree of heat applied to the vessel.</note> whose heat dried
The waters more and more;
And also <note>original: "eke"</note> a central heat <note>The internal heat of the matter itself, often compared to a fire in the center of the earth.</note> beneath did fry them,
And sent up a great store of vapors.

Which continued to return, until they were changed—
The greater part of them to dust;
The wandering souls, which had long been estranged,
Were now enticed with a longing <note>original: "allur'd with lust"</note>

For their own bodies, in whose atoms lay
A strange magnetic force;
They also, though long banished, day by day
Awaited the dead corpse. <note>original: "Coarse"</note>

# Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate. <note>Ripley’s "Twelve Gates" is a famous 15th-century alchemical poem; the Fourth Gate usually refers to the stage of "Solution."</note>

*For souls united and bodies combined,*
*And both to each allied*
*So closely, that no sooner can they find*
*A way, but they are tied*

*With an unbreakable <note>original: "inviolable"</note> knot, that no power,*
*However strong it may appear,*
*Can part them anymore, even from the hour*
*They were thus united.*

*Thus two share one body, of double sex, <note>This refers to the "Hermaphrodite" or "Rebis," a central alchemical symbol representing the perfect union of opposites, such as sulfur and mercury or male and female principles.</note>*
*Which no sooner begins to live*
*But it is impregnated; this does perplex*
*Their foes, who would gladly drive*

*This tender offspring to despair: but God*
*Keeps him safely forever*
*From all invasion, nor permits the rod*
*Of those enemies to make him weep.*

*And though his garments and his skin be foul <note>The OCR lists "(soul" at the end of the line, but "foul" is the likely intended word to rhyme with "soul" and describe the "stink" of the nigredo stage.</note>*
*With blackness and with a stench, <note>This describes the "Nigredo" or black stage of alchemy, where the matter undergoes putrefaction and emits a foul odor before being purified.</note>*
*He shall be purified, <note>original: "purged"</note> for both spirit and soul*
*Are clean, whatever men think.*

*A river springs up in the middle of a beautiful garden,*
*Adorned with many flowers,*
*Whose drops are like crystal; these into the air*
*Are drawn up <note>original: "checkt"</note> by central heat.* <note>The "central heat" refers to the internal fire of the alchemical vessel that causes the substance to evaporate and circulate.</note>

This air condenses like an eastern pearl, <note>original: "Pearl Orient"; a term for high-quality pearls from the East, symbolizing purity and the beginning of the "white" stage.</note>
Which falls upon this body;
Whose brilliance, spent upon the blackness,
Recalls it back to brightness.

And both together make a crystal spring,
Whose streams shine most strangely;
These are afterward condensed, and they bring
With them treasures of fine silver. <note>This represents the *Albedo*, or the white stage of the alchemical work, where the matter is purified into "philosophical silver."</note>

If these treasures are left to rest untouched 
For a while upon the fire,
The creeping blue <note>original: "Azure"</note> will deceive the white,
And both will be deprived

Of their existence by the green, <term>Vert</term> <note>original: "Vert"; the green stage, sometimes called the "Green Lion," often represents a middle stage of acidity or growth in the chemical reaction.</note> which will endure for a long time;

To see the sparkling red. <note>The *Rubedo*, or red stage, is the final phase of the Great Work, signifying the perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

Then the King, who was formerly drowned, <note>The "drowned King" refers to the gold or sulfur that was dissolved in the earlier "Solution" stage.</note>
Becomes the wonder of the whole earth;
His wife and he are one, and both abound
With wealth; nor will they ever again

Depart from one another: now all their enemies
Must acknowledge their dominion;
Those who will not bow shall surely feel their blows,
For all are beneath him.

To have neither wisdom nor fear.

# The Fifth Gate Opened,
## Which is
# PUTREFACTION.

*Now we begin the Chapter of Putrefaction, 
Without which pivot <note>original: "Pole"; referring to a central axis or an essential turning point in the process</note> no seed can multiply, 
Which must be done only by the continual action 
Of heat in the body—moist, but not manipulated by hand <note>original: "manually"; the author emphasizes that this must be a natural chemical process rather than a mechanical one</note>; 
For otherwise, bodies cannot be altered naturally.*

We have already passed through four gates; since the first was opened, the rest stand open at your will, so that you need only enter. The course and method of the philosophers now leads us to the Gate of Putrefaction—a horrible gate, whose entrance is dark as Cimmerian <note>A reference to the Cimmerians of Greek mythology, who were said to live in a land of perpetual mist and darkness.</note> night, and terrifying with many twists and turns.

And yet it is a gate so necessary that unless you pass through it, you can never expect to reap the fruits of your la— <meta>catchword: bours</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the fifth stage of the alchemical process, Putrefaction, describing it as a dark but essential phase of natural decay required for any further progress. The author emphasizes that this stage must occur through consistent, natural heat rather than manual intervention.</summary>
<keywords>Putrefaction, Fifth Gate, Cimmerian night, alchemical seed, natural alteration, philosophical method</keywords>

bour; for without it there can be no life nor generation, much less the desired multiplication. Therefore, the poet says,

->*Happy are those, as many as can have the dark state.* <note>original: "Felices atrum quotquot habere queant"; "atrum" refers to the black or dark stage of the alchemical process, known as the nigredo.</note><-

The cause of this death, corruption, or rottenness proceeds from the action of continual heat—not so much the external fire of the <term>athanor</term> <note>athanor: a furnace used by alchemists, designed to provide a steady, uniform heat over a long period</note>, as the compound within itself. In this compound, the "fire of the water" (which is against nature) opens the perfect body by continual grinding <note>original: "contrition"</note> and boiling <note>original: "decoction"</note>. This releases its <term>sulphur</term> <note>sulphur: in alchemy, one of the three prime principles (Tria Prima), representing the active, masculine, or combustible principle</note> that was imprisoned <note>original: "incarcerate"</note>, which is the "fire of nature." Thus, through the continual action and interaction <note>original: "passion"</note> between these two, along with the external heat acting continually, the whole compound is brought to corruption. It is sometimes roasted with external heat, which causes the moisture to rise <note>original: "sublime"</note>; this moisture then returns continually of its own accord and moistens the "earth" <note>the solid matter at the bottom of the vessel</note> until, by reason of the heat, it has drunk up the moisture entirely—and then it dies.

...in a black color, a stinking odor, and with a separation <note>original: "discontinuity"</note> of parts, your labor will still be in vain; for you may never expect what you desire—to have a new form introduced—until the old form is corrupted and cast off.

Since Christ testifies that unless the grain of wheat
Dies in the ground, you can get no increase.
And likewise, without the <term>matter putrefying</term> <note>original: "putrefie"; the process of decay or decomposition necessary for transformation</note>,
It can in no way truly be changed <note>original: "alterate"</note>,
Neither may your <term>elements</term> <note>the four classical elements: earth, air, fire, and water</note> be divided naturally <note>original: "kindly"</note>,
Nor their <term>conjunction</term> <note>the union of opposites, such as the alchemical "marriage" of sulfur and mercury</note> be perfectly celebrated.
Therefore, so that your labor is not frustrated,
Understand the secret <note>original: "privity"</note> of our putrefying well
Before you ever take this <term>Work</term> <note>the "Great Work" or *Magnum Opus* of alchemy</note> in hand.

Once he has arrived there, he is as surely a Master as if he already possessed the **Stone** <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

# Sir G. Ripley's fifth Gate.

...the **Bodies**, and this seeming **Corruption** <note>original: "Corruption"; in alchemy, the breakdown of matter, often called the "death" of the metal</note>, was but the natural step to a glorious **Regeneration**. For if the **Souls** are again united to the **Bodies**, they will surely regenerate them, renew them, and make them incorruptible. If the **Spirits** had not returned to this union, they might only have expected them to evaporate <note>original: "Exhalation"</note>; for spiritual **Tinctures** <note>original: "Tinctures"; the essential, concentrated qualities or coloring principles of the substance</note> or **Essences** cannot be destroyed by corruption in many **plants** <note>original: "Vegetals"</note>, much less in the most perfect of minerals and metals. The union, then, of the **Souls** with the **Bodies** clearly proves a hidden purity beneath the apparent rottenness, which will—after purification—be raised to supreme glory.

Based on this, they said that the **Spirit** and **Soul** which were above were the **Heavenly Quintessence** <note>original: "Quinteſſence"; the "fifth element," the purest and most essential part of a substance</note>, and the **Body** below was the **Earth**. This **Circulation of Spirits** <note>the movement of vapors rising and falling within the alchemical vessel</note> was just like the circulation of the heavens around the Earth. The falling drops were like the influential dew which caused the Earth to bear fruit <note>original: "fructifie"</note>. Furthermore, the blackness and darkness were merely the **Winter Latitude** <note>a metaphorical term for the "winter" phase of the work, representing a cold, dark, and dormant period</note>, which...

### Sir George Ripley's Fifth Gate

Therefore, what philosophers have discovered and seen through long experience and deep meditation, they have also entrusted and communicated to us; so that we, too, are made fellow heirs of this great treasure, and we shall just as faithfully communicate our knowledge for your instruction. First, then, take your "Body," which is *gold*, and your "Water," which is *mercury*. The one is made ready for you by Nature, but the other you must prepare yourself, for it is not to be found in the land of the living in its necessary state, but must be manufactured. In this task, Nature alone is at a standstill <note>original: "non-plus"; a state where no further progress can be made</note>, and so is Art when they are taken separately; but both working together effect the goal.

Mix these together in the proper proportions, just as I have often told you. Then set them over the fire to digest <note>original: "decoct"; the process of heating a substance to refine it or extract its essence</note>, and apply a suitable heat in which they may boil, rise, and fall <note>referring to the alchemical process of circulation, where vapors rise and condense back into the liquid perpetually</note> without any interruption, day or night.

But especially, and above all things, be careful regarding your "internal heat"—that is to say, the proportion of your Water to your *sulphur* <term>Sulphur: In alchemy, this often represents the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance, contrasted with the fluid Mercury</term>. You must add and supply to it...

<summary>This page explains that the alchemist must combine natural gold with a specially prepared mercury, maintaining a constant cycle of evaporation and condensation through steady heat. It emphasizes that the success of the work depends on the "internal heat" created by the correct ratio of ingredients.</summary>

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Fifth Gate, Gold, Mercury, Decoction, Internal Heat, Sulphur, Alchemical Proportions</keywords>

...it in the beginning of your work, in its preparation, is what performs all the work within. Without this, your external heat is of no value, for it is ineffective by itself.

If you then ignite <note>original: "accend"</note> this heat so much that it becomes dominant, it will not dissolve the "Bodies" as you expect, but will instead burn the "Flowers" <note>the refined essences that rise as vapor during heating</note> before they are extracted from the depths of their "Marrow" <note>their innermost core or essence</note>. You might easily do this if your *arsenic* is not prepared as it should be, or if the number of "Eagles" <term>Eagles: An alchemical term for the number of times mercury has been distilled or purified; it measures the "strength" of the mercurial solvent</term> is exceeded, or if the proportion of your "Water" to your "Body" does not agree with the number of "Eagles," or if your glass vessel is not well-proportioned to your material. It will easily burn if your glass is too large, for the moisture will be so widely dispersed around the concave interior that it will not return <note>re-condense into liquid</note> before the "Earth" below is left too dry. I have given rules to easily avoid all these inconveniences.

And on the other hand, be sure that you do not err by using too little heat. Let your "Water" have enough fire within it to create a true division and corruption <note>the essential alchemical stage where the matter breaks down so it can be transformed</note>, which

...which happens if either your *arsenic* <note>In this context, "arsenic" refers to a specific mercurial solvent or "secret fire," not necessarily the common poison</note> lacks sufficient fiery power <note>original: "virtue"</note>, or if the union of this and your Water is not well-attended but poorly <note>original: "slightly"</note> performed, or if the purification <note>original: "Purgation"</note> of your Water is not thoroughly completed for each Eagle <term>Eagle: An alchemical term for the number of times mercury has been distilled or purified; it measures the "strength" of the mercurial solvent</term>. This failure can also occur if your number of Eagles is not exact, or if the proportion of quantity is not properly observed.

Continue this until the vapors cease and are held within the vessel; then the compound will decay <note>original: "rot"</note>, which, because of its great resemblance to the heat of decomposition, is called our "dunghill."

### Sir George Ripley's Fifth Gate.

Natures now begin to embrace and kiss one another.

For as soon as they feel the fire, they flow together within the vessel and visibly boil through continuous heating <note>original: "decoction"</note>. The tender Nature, unable to endure the heat, flies upward, and being enclosed so that it cannot escape, it congeals into drops in the head of the vessel and along the sides, and then returns again to its body. This may well be called "children’s play" <note>This refers to *Ludus Puerorum*, a stage in alchemy where the process becomes self-sustaining and requires little effort from the chemist</note>, as it runs around in a circular motion. This play continues until the water begins to leave its thicker parts, along with the thicker parts of the body; the liquid in the bottom of the vessel is then called *Urina puerorum* <note>Latin: "boys' urine," a symbolic term for the purified liquid or alkaline solution used in the work</note>. The thinner parts of the water, mixed with the thinner parts of the body dissolved within it, continue to fly upward and circulate until they have achieved a more complete dissolution of the body. Here, through the scent <note>original: "odour"</note> of its *Sulfur*, this mixture penetrates the spirit and soul, making them faint at last and remain breathless, as it were, in the bottom of the glass.

Then the body shall be destroyed, and both
<sig>Y 2 both</sig>

<summary>This page describes the "Fifth Gate" or sublimation process, where heat causes the alchemical substances to circulate as vapor and condensation, a stage metaphorically called "children's play." The process leads to the dissolution of the "body" as it is penetrated by the "spirit."</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Fifth Gate, sublimation, decoction, children's play, Urina puerorum, sulfur, circulation, alchemical marriage</keywords>

### Sir G. Ripley's *fifth* Gate.

[dissol]ved Body let loose, which mixing with the <term>Sulphur</term> of the Water, does sharpen <note>original: "acuate"; to make more reactive, potent, or corrosive</note> it exceedingly. The one being a natural fire and the other a "Fire against Nature," both together make an unnatural Fire, burning like the Fire of Hell, comparable to nothing but the <term>Alcahest</term> <note>The *Alcahest* was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, said to be capable of reducing any substance to its primary matter.</note>.

Nor must you think that this increase of Fire consists in the blowing of the coal; no indeed, it is a more subtle internal fire that we have. Yet, that internal fire also must be kept constant and in due order.

For this reason, see that your furnace is reliable <note>original: "trusty"</note>, otherwise you may and will fail. For although the fire of coals does not effect the transformation itself, it excites the process; and the Water, though it is of a wonderful nature, acts no further than it is stirred up by heat. Any interruption in this work, once it is begun, will in the end prove to be a fatal extinction.

Therefore the Wise men have named the furnace in which they work their secrets an <term>Athanor</term>—that is, "Immortal" <note>derived from the Greek *athanatos* (undying); the name refers to the furnace’s ability to maintain a steady, continuous heat for long periods without the fire going out</note>—showing that from the beginning to the end the fire must not go out, for the...

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical transformation is driven by a combination of "natural" and "unnatural" fires, emphasizing the need for a reliable Athanor furnace to maintain constant heat and prevent the process from dying out.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, Alcahest, Athanor, furnace, internal fire, fire against nature, George Ripley, putrefaction</keywords>

--- Page 414 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Athanor" or "Immortal Furnace," emphasizing the necessity of maintaining a constant, temperate heat to ensure the successful decomposition (putrefaction) of the alchemical matter.</meta>

<page-num>340</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

The extinction of the fire destroys the Work; and just as death includes all illnesses, which are steps toward it, so an "Immortal Furnace" or <term>Athanor</term> <note>original: *Athanor*; derived from the Greek *athanatos* (undying), this was a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant temperature for long periods</note> must not only prevent the fire from going out, but also keep it from becoming excessive in one direction or the other. For whatever deviates <note>original: "serves," likely a printer's error for "swerves"</note> from the temperate middle ground hinders the natural operation <note>original: "kind operation"</note> of the Matter, which is <term>Putrefaction</term> <note>the stage of the alchemical process where the substance must "die" or rot to be purified and reborn</note>. By such deviation, the Work is significantly delayed and weakened, and if any extreme temperature continues, the Work will be destroyed; but with its proper heat, the Matter decomposes naturally.

*Of this principle speaks the wise <term>Guido</term>* <note>likely Guido de Montanor, a famous alchemical authority</note>,
*And says that through rotting, the physical compound dies,*
*And then, according to <term>Morien</term>* <note>Morienus, a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist</note> *and many others,*
*It rises again—regenerate, simple, and spiritual.*
*And if heat and moisture were not constant,*
*Sperm could not remain in the womb,*
*And so no fruit would grow from it.*

This is in accordance with the intention of all the Philosophers—<term>Guido</term>, the authors of the <term>Turba</term> <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note>, and <term>Arnaldus</term> <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a renowned physician and alchemist</note>...

<summary>This page explains that a constant, temperate heat is vital for the alchemical process of putrefaction, comparing the necessity of this heat to the conditions required for biological life and growth. It cites various alchemical authorities to support the idea that the "physical compound" must rot before it can be spiritualized.</summary>
<keywords>Athanor, Putrefaction, Guido de Montanor, Morienus, Turba Philosophorum, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, alchemical fire, spiritualization</keywords>

## Sir G. Ripley's Fifth Gate.

...dus <note>Continuing from the previous page: likely "Guido," referring to Guido de Montanor</note> and others, but especially the noble *Trevisan* <note>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist known for his search for the Philosopher's Stone</note>, whom I chiefly honor; so *Flammel* <note>Nicolas Flamel, the legendary French alchemist</note>, *Artephius*, *Morien*, and all *Philosophers* testify to this: namely, that the heat must be so balanced <note>original: "adequated"</note> to the Compound that within it, the Body—through the acidic power <note>original: "Pontick virtue"; "Pontick" refers to a sharp, transformative, or "biting" quality in alchemical waters</note> of the Water—may have its *Sulphur* released. Thus, these two *Sulphurs* <note>representing the active principles of both the "body" and the "spirit" of the matter</note> mixing together may bring the whole substance to rotting or *Putrefaction*.

By this state of decay, a ferment is produced. Just as this ferment naturally causes all things to become volatile <note>to turn into vapor or gas</note>, it also brings life to <note>original: "quicken"</note> this coarse, dead Body. It rises upward above the Fire along with the Water, emerging as a new, glorious Body mixed with the Water. Because both have become one together, the Spirit borrows permanence from the Body, and the Body gains the ability to penetrate other substances <note>original: "penetrativeness"</note> from the Spirit. Together, they create a single celestial and terrestrial Compound, named the "Regenerate Body" and the "Incombustible Stone of Paradise." All of this is caused by the constant and steady application of heat, both internally and externally.

<summary>This page explains how balanced heat allows the alchemical "Body" and "Water" to interact, leading to putrefaction and the eventual creation of a "Regenerate Body" or the Stone of Paradise. This process unites the volatile spirit with the stable body, granting the final substance both permanence and the power to transform other materials.</summary>

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Bernard Trevisan, Putrefaction, Sulphur, Fermentation, Regenerate Body, Stone of Paradise, alchemical heat, Volatilization</keywords>

<page-num>342</page-num>

—truly, by which the moisture is circulated and purified <note>original: "depurated"</note>. Without this circulation, the feminine essence <note>original: "feminal virtue"; in alchemy, this refers to the passive, receptive, or mercurial principle</note> would be destroyed, for it only lives and grows <note>original: "vegetates"</note> through heat and moisture.

And if once the feminine essence were killed, the remaining mixture <note>original: "Compound"</note> would be no better than a dead, useless thing which could never be restored. So, if either the internal moisture or heat, or the appropriate <note>original: "convenient"</note> external heat should fail, there is nothing to be expected but what the poet says:
> *All things will fall to ruin, and you will not be able to repair them by any art.* <note>original Latin: "Cuncta ruent, quæ non ulla reparaveris Arte"</note>

*Therefore, at the beginning, take our Stone,*

*Once their seed is conceived, nourish and save it naturally <note>original: "kindly"</note>,*
*Ensuring it does not rise from the bottom of its grave during that time—*
*A secret point that deceives <note>original: "beguile"</note> many people.*

**T**his then is the process of our Work: at the start, take our Stone—that is, the...

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the "Fifth Gate," focusing on the union of the two primary materials—the masculine and feminine principles—into a single substance, leading to the stage of blackness.</meta>

<page-num>343</page-num>
<header>Sir G. Ripley's Fifth Gate</header>

...the true material principles of it, which are one in species <note>original: "kind"</note> and two in number. Mix these together in a proper proportion, and then you shall see the following:

First, your feminine nature will so embrace your masculine nature that she will extract his seed from him—that is, the most refined <note>original: "digested"</note> power <note>original: "virtue"</note>. In this way, the Body shall die, and the Water shall entomb it.

Through living together <note>original: "Cohabitation"</note>, the Water shall form a bond of friendship with the Body, for the Water is nothing other than a feminine body of the same origin <note>original: "Stock"</note>. When they are united and joined, this is called the marriage of *Gabritius* <note>Gabritius (or Gabricus) represents the masculine principle, often associated with sulfur or gold</note> with *Beya* <note>Beya represents the feminine principle, often associated with mercury or silver; their "marriage" is a central alchemical allegory for the union of opposites</note>.

Then the vapors shall cease, and all exhalations shall be prevented from rising or falling for a period of 40 days, or 42 at most. During this time, even though you do not see the previous circulation <note>The process of liquid evaporating and condensing</note>, rejoice; for now the Body begins to hold its Soul in a black color <note>This "black color" refers to the *nigredo* stage, where the matter decomposes and putrefies before it can be reborn and purified</note>.

Now the Queen has conceived the kingly seed, which must be nourished with a gentle, appropriate <note>original: "convenient"</note> fire until it grows strong.

<summary>This page describes the "marriage" of the masculine and feminine principles (Gabritius and Beya) and the subsequent period of stillness and blackness (putrefaction) necessary for the alchemical seed to grow.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Fifth Gate, Gabritius, Beya, alchemical marriage, nigredo, putrefaction, masculine and feminine principles</keywords>

--- Page 418 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the transformation of the alchemical materials during the "blackness" or nigredo stage, explaining how the opposing elemental qualities reach a state of reconciliation.</meta>

<page-num>344</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

...strong, and become a powerful, triumphant champion, whom no earthly force is able to withstand.

You must necessarily allow them this predetermined time for their solitude and retirement. During this time, the fixed and the volatile, the cold and the moist, the hot and the dry, learn to agree with one another. They are reconciled in this "Head of the Crow" <term>The *Head of the Crow* (Latin: *Caput Corvi*) is the traditional name for the *nigredo* stage, a period of blackness and decomposition necessary for spiritual and physical transformation.</term>, which is the transformation of natures into a fine powder <note>original: "discontinuous Calx"</note>, finer than dust motes dancing in a sunbeam <note>original: "Atoms of the Sun"</note>.

This operation is a wilderness and a labyrinth to all who seek this Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy or the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in vain. However, it is the Cape of Good Hope <note>original: "*Capo di Bona Esperanza*"</note> to all those who attain a perfect understanding of it. For by this point, most of the difficulties they are likely to encounter in this voyage to the "East Indies" <note>The "Oriental Indies" serves as an allegory for the ultimate prize of the alchemical process: the Philosopher's Stone and the wealth and wisdom it brings.</note> are now passed.

<summary>This page describes the reconciliation of opposing elemental qualities during the "Head of the Crow" (nigredo) stage, marking it as the most difficult but hopeful turning point in the alchemical process.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for managing the heat during the "blackness" stage, warning the alchemist against overheating the mixture and describing the visual signs of successful putrefaction.</meta>

<header>Sir George Ripley's Fifth Gate</header>

*Wait through this time of conception with gentle heat,*
*The appearing blackness shall tell you when they die,*
*For together, like a tide of liquid pitch,*
*They shall swell and bubble, settle and putrefy;*
*Shining colors you shall see within,*
*Like the rainbow, marvelous to behold,*
*Then the water begins to dry up completely.*

You must then be very careful that your excessive heat does not now hinder their union <note>original: "Conjunction"</note>, for now the main fear is of burning your "flowers" <term>In alchemy, "flowers" refers to the volatile spirits or the delicate substances that rise and collect at the top of the vessel during heating.</term>, which you might easily do. This would make these "natures" <note>The "natures" refer to the two primary opposing substances (often represented as the King and Queen or Sulfur and Mercury) being combined.</note> become a dull red or orange color, instead of the true "Crow’s Bill" <term>The "Crow's Bill" or "Crow's Head" is the traditional symbol for the *nigredo* stage, representing the deepest blackness of total decomposition.</term>.

However, if your external heat is so gentle that it does not stop the internal motion, you shall find that in this period both of your "natures" shall die together; for one is not killed, nor does it die, without the other. You shall discover this "death" as it approaches by the appearance of blackness.

And once the "Crow" shall begin to show itself, know that you shall see—
